A tale of parahoric–Hecke algebras, Bernstein and Satake homomorphisms. The author was supported by the Swiss National Science Foundation grant #P2ELP2-191672.
Reda Boumasmoud

Abstract
. Let 𝐆 \mathbf{G} be a connected reductive group over a non-archimedean local field F F . Let K ℱ K_{\mathcal{F}} be the parahoric subgroup attached to a facet ℱ \mathcal{F} in the Bruhat–Tits building of 𝐆 \mathbf{G} . The ultimate goal of the present paper is to describe the center of the parahoric–Hecke algebra ℋ ⁡ ( 𝐆 ⁡ ( F ) ⫽ K ℱ , ℤ ⁡ [ q − 1 ] ) \mathcal{H}(\mathbf{G}(F)\sslash{K_{\mathcal{F}}},\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]) with level K ℱ K_{\mathcal{F}} and prove the compatibility of generalized (twisted) Bernstein and Satake homomorphisms.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
11E95, 20E42, 20G25 and 20C08 (primary).

1.
Introduction

Congruences between modular forms, automorphic forms or automorphic representations have received a significant attention in the past few decades for several reasons.
Their study has helped investigating numerous questions that have been wandering in number theory’s paysage, e.g. first and foremost, Andrew Wiles’ proof of Fermat’s last theorem.

An omnipresent theme occurring in this landscape are Hecke algebras.
These are as fundamental as Galois theory is.
Actually, in more than one respect, the two go hand in hand.
These special algebras are concerned with the following kind of situation. We are given a reductive group
\mathbf{G}
defined over a
p
-adic field
F
and
a smooth
1
1

1

Every vector
v\in V
has an open stabilizer in
\mathbf{G}(F)
.
complex representation of
\mathbf{G}(F)
, say
(V,r)
. If
C\subset\mathbf{G}(F)
is an open compact subgroup, then we introduce the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{G}(F)\sslash C,\mathds{C})
, which is the convolution of double cosets in
C\backslash\mathbf{G}(F)/C
.
Similar to how the group algebra of a finite group allows us to translate representation theory questions into module-theoretic ones,
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{G}(F)\sslash C,\mathds{C})
provides a mechanism that allows us to dissect the representations
(V,r)
.
In more concrete terms, we have a bijection between the set of isomorphism classes of irreducible smooth complex
\mathbf{G}(F)
-representations
V
such that
V^{C}\neq 0
and the set of isomorphism classes of simple smooth
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{G}(F)\sslash C,\mathds{C})
-modules.

The purpose of this process is to obtain algebraic or number theoretical information which may yield new insight about
\mathbf{G}(F)
or about its representations theory.
Therefore, it is decisive to further deepen our understanding of Hecke algebras and classify their categories of modules.

An important step toward this goal is offered by Satake’s transform, which yields an explicit description of
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{G}(F)\sslash C,\mathds{C})
for two crucial cases: maximal open compact subgroups by Satake
[
Sat63
]
and special maximal parahoric subgroups by Haines–Rostami
[
HR10
]
.
Besides, Hecke algebras for smaller levels also hold a special place and have intriguing applications, for instance when
C=K_{\mathcal{F}}
is a parahoric subgroup, Pappas and Zhu proved in
[
PZ13
]
a conjecture of Kottwitz which asserts that the semi-simple trace of Frobenius on the nearby cycles of a Shimura variety gives a function which is central in
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{G}(F)\sslash K_{\mathcal{F}},\mathds{C})
.
Thanks to the characterization of the minuscule central Bernstein basis functions by Haines
[
Hai01
]
, one can actually compute these traces.
However, there remains the task of comprehending the center of these parahoric Hecke algebras.
Such an understanding, is provided by the framework of Bernstein presentations for Iwahori–Hecke algebra when
C=I
is an Iwahori subgroup, which is due to
[
Lus89
]
for the extended affine case and more recently to Rostami
[
Ros15
]
and Vigneras
[
Vig16
]
for the general reductive case.

This paper attempts to extend and generalize to general reductive
p
-adic groups various results known for Hecke algebras for split, unramified or semi-simple and simply connected
p
-adic groups.
In this direction, we are particularly interested in the study of these Hecke algebras when their ring of coefficients is as small as possible;
\mathds{Z}
or
\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]
.
Our methods are of geometric flavor and involve extensively Bruhat–Tits theory.
They provide a uniform treatment for Hecke algebras with parahoric levels and their
\flat
-avatars (§
3.1
).
This allows us to take full advantage of Lusztig’s work
[
Lus89
]
and extend his results to the general reductive case.
Following the ideas of Bernstein
[
BD84
]
,
we focus our attention on the universal unramified principal series module, which leads us naturally to the Bernstein elements
\dot{\Theta}_{m}
and accordingly the Bernstein presentation of the Iwahori–Hecke algebras.
The payoff for this, we obtain an explicit description of the center of general parahoric Hecke algebras with coefficients in
\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]
, and also integral twisted Satake isomorphism thus generalizing
[
HR10
]
and
[
Sat63
, §9]
together with compatibilities between integral twisted Satake isomorphism and twisted Bernstein isomorphism.
Along the way, we also prove various auxiliary results that may be useful in other contexts. Furthermore, we give as much as we can original proofs for all the previously known results.

A hidden motivation for this paper, is to prepare the ground field for introducing and studying the ring of
\mathbb{U}
-operators, which is the subject of the sequel part II of this paper
[
Bou21e
]
.
This is the first step of an ongoing project exploring arithmetic application of these operators in Euler systems theory
[
Bou21a
,
Bou21b
]
and Eichler–Shimura congruence relations for general Shimura varieties
[
Bou21d
,
Bou21c
]
.

1.1.
Main results

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a reductive group defined over a non-archimedean local field
F
.
Let
\mathbf{S}
be maximal
F
-split subtorus of
\mathbf{G}
and
\mathcal{A}
its corresponding apartment in the reduced Bruhat–Tits building of
\mathbf{G}
.
Set
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{N}
for the centralizer and the normalizer of
\mathbf{S}
in
\mathbf{G}
, respectively. We have a homomorphism of groups
\nu_{N}\colon\mathbf{N}(F)\to\textit{\emph{\text{Aff}}}(\mathcal{A})
.
Let
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{A}
be an alcove, and
a_{\circ}\in\overline{\mathfrak{a}}
a special point.
Set
I\subset K
for the Iwahori subgroup and the special maximal parahoric subgroup corresponding to
{\mathfrak{a}}
and
a_{\circ}
, respectively.

Let
M_{1}=\ker\kappa_{M}
and
G_{1}=\ker\kappa_{G}
be the kernels of the Kottwitz map for
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{G}
(see §
2.8.2
).
Fix any open compact subgroup
M_{1}\subset M^{\flat}\subset M
.
By
[
Lan96
, Proposition 1.2]
,
M^{\flat}
is contained in the maximal open compact subgroup
M^{1}
.

For any
X\subset G
, set
X^{\flat}=M^{\flat}X
. Whenever we use this notation, the structure of the object we are interested in is not altered, e.g.
K^{\flat}
or
G_{1}^{\flat}
are still a groups.

For any facet
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{A}
, set
K_{\mathcal{F}}
for the parahoric subgroup attached to it,
N_{1,\mathcal{F}}:=\mathbf{N}(F)\cap K_{\mathcal{F}}
and
W_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}=N_{1,\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}/M^{\flat}
. Because
a_{\circ}
is special,
W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}\simeq W:=\mathbf{M}(F)/\mathbf{N}(F)
.

Set
\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}:=\mathbf{M}(F)/M^{\flat}
,
\Lambda_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}:=(\mathbf{M}(F)\cap G_{1})^{\flat}/M^{\flat}
,
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}:=\mathds{Z}[\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}]
and
\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}:=\mathds{Z}[\Lambda_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}]
.

We write
\dot{W}
, for the Weyl group to specify that it acts on
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
by the dot-action, this is the standard action twisted by a factor coming form the modulus function (see Definition
3.16.1
).

If
A
is a commutative ring and
H\subset\mathbf{G}(F)
an open compact subgroup, define
\mathcal{H}_{H}(A)
to be the
A
-algebra of functions
f\colon\mathbf{G}(F)\to A
, that are
H
-invariant on the right and the left.

1.1.1.
The Iwahori–Matsumoto presentation

We start with a well known result, at least for Iwahori–Hecke attached to extended affine Weyl groups. See for example
[
Gar97
, §6.3]
.
For the general case, it was showed in
[
Vig16
, Theorem 2.1]
or
[
Ros15
, Proposition 4.1.1]
with
\mathds{C}
-coefficients.
We will reprove it here using a different argument.

Theorem
3.13.1

(The Iwahori–Matsumoto Presentation)
.

The Iwahori–Hecke ring
\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
is the free
\mathds{Z}
-module with basis
\{i_{w}={\bf 1}_{I^{\flat}wI^{\flat}},{w\in\widetilde{W}^{\flat}}:=N/M^{\flat}\}
, endowed with the unique ring structure satisfying

•

The braid relations:

i_{w}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w^{\prime}}^{\flat}=i_{ww^{\prime}}^{\flat}\text{ if }w,w^{\prime}\in\widetilde{W}^{\flat}\text{ such that }\ell(w)+\ell(w^{\prime})=\ell(ww^{\prime})
.

•

The quadratic relations:

(i_{s}^{\flat})^{2}=q_{s}i_{1}^{\flat}+(q_{s}-1)i_{s}^{\flat}\text{ if }s\in\mathcal{S}^{\flat}
, where
q_{s}:=[IsI:I]
and
\mathcal{S}^{\flat}\subset\widetilde{W}^{\flat}
corresponds to the set of orthogonal reflections with respect to the walls of the fixed alcove.

1.1.2.
Freeness of the universal unramified principal series module

Let
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{A}
be any fixed facet.
For any commutative ring
A
, define the universal unramified principal series right
(\mathcal{R}^{\flat},\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(A))
-bi-module
\mathcal{M}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(A)=\mathcal{C}_{c}(M^{\flat}U^{+}\backslash G/K_{\mathcal{F}},A)
, this is the set of
A
-valued functions supported on finitely many double cosets.

Proposition
4.1.4
.

The
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
-module
\mathcal{M}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
is free of rank
r_{\mathcal{F}}:=|W/\jmath_{W}(W_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat})|
, with basis
\{v_{w,\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}:={\bf 1}_{U^{+}wK_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}},\,w\in D_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}\}
where
D_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}\subset W_{a_{\circ}}
is any fixed set of representatives for
W/\jmath_{W}(W_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat})
and
\jmath_{W}\colon N\to W=N/M
is the quotient map.
In particular,
\{v_{w,\mathfrak{a}}^{\flat},\,w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}\}
is a canonical basis for the
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
-module
\mathcal{M}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
.

1.1.3.
Bernstein presentation of the Iwahori–Hecke algebra

Following the ideas of Bernstein
[
BD84
]
, as exposed by the approach of
[
HKP10
]
(which treats the split case), we use the universal unramified principal series module
\mathcal{M}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
to study the Iwahori–Hecke algebra.
A first result into this direction is:

Theorem
4.2.3
.

The following homomorphism of right
\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])
-modules is an isomorphism

\displaystyle\hbar_{I^{\flat}}\colon\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}),\quad h\longmapsto v_{1,\mathfrak{a}}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}h.

In §
4.3
, we construct an embedding
\dot{\Theta}_{Bern}^{\flat}\colon\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]),m\mapsto\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}
. This provides an alternative description for the Iwahori–Hecke algebra:

Corollary
4.3.6
.

The algebra
\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
is a free left (and right)
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}{\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
-module, with canonical basis
\{i_{w}^{\flat}\colon w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}\}
.
The sets
\{\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}*_{{I^{\flat}}}i_{w}^{\flat}\colon m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat},w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}\}

and
\{i_{w}^{\flat}*_{{I^{\flat}}}\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}\colon m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat},w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}\}
are both
{\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
-basis for
\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
.

Set
\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}(\mathds{Z}):=\mathcal{H}(G_{1}^{\flat}\sslash I^{\flat},\mathds{Z})
and let
\widetilde{\Upomega}^{\flat}
denote the subgroup of stabilizers of
\mathfrak{a}
in
\widetilde{W}^{\flat}
.
The following diagram is commutative (see Proposition
3.13.4
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{I}(\mathds{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{Z}[\widetilde{\Upomega}]\otimes^{\prime}\mathcal{H}_{I}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}(\mathds{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{Z}[\widetilde{\Upomega}^{\flat}]\otimes^{\prime}\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}(\mathds{Z})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square^{\flat}\otimes\iota^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where,
\otimes^{\prime}
indicates that the tensor modules is endowed with a ”untwisted” multiplication:
(\sigma\otimes i_{w})(\sigma^{\prime}\otimes i_{w^{\prime}})=\sigma\sigma^{\prime}\otimes i_{\sigma^{\prime-1}w\sigma^{\prime}}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w^{\prime}}
for any
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\Upomega}^{\flat}
and any
w,w^{\prime}\in\widetilde{W}^{\flat}
.
The restriction of the surjective
\iota^{\flat}
to
\mathcal{H}_{I}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}(\mathds{Z})
is injective (Remark
3.3.4
) and yields an isomorphism of algebras
\mathcal{H}_{I}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}(\mathds{Z})\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}}\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}(\mathds{Z})
.

This fact, allows us to extend various results due to Lusztig in
[
Lus89
]
for the affine extended case (
M^{\flat}=M^{1}
) to the general reductive case:

Proposition
4.4.3
.

For any
s\in\mathcal{T}_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}=\mathcal{S}^{\flat}\cap W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}
and any
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
, we have

\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}(i_{s}^{\flat}+1)-(i_{s}^{\flat}+1)*_{I^{\flat}}\dot{\Theta}_{\dot{s}(m)}^{\flat}=(\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}-\dot{\Theta}_{\dot{s}(m)}^{\flat})\mathcal{G}(\alpha)\in\mathcal{R}^{\flat}

where,
\mathcal{G}(\alpha)\in\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}
is the element defined in
[
Lus89
, §3.8 & §3.13]
for the algebra
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{D}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}},{W}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}},v=q,L)
(see
3.14
).

This result was also obtained by Rostami
[
Ros15
, Proposition 5.4.2]
for the untwisted
\Theta
-elements, using a slightly different argument.

1.1.4.
Twisted Satake isomorphism with
\mathds{Z}
-coefficients

Motivated by arithmetic problems for Shimura varieties, Haines and Rostami established in
[
HR10
]
,
\mathcal{S}_{M}^{G}\colon\mathcal{H}_{K}(\mathds{C})\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}}\mathcal{R}^{W}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{C}
for the untwisted Satake morphism.
In §
5
, considering the twisted Satake isomorphism, allows us to extend their result to the Hecke algebra with integral coefficients
\mathcal{H}_{K}(\mathds{Z})
.

The twisted Satake transform
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}\colon\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}\to\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
is characterized by
v_{1,a_{\circ}}^{\flat}*_{K^{\flat}}h=\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}(h)\cdot v_{1,a_{\circ}}^{\flat},\text{ for all }{h\in\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})}
.

Theorem
5.2.1
.

The twisted Satake homomorphism
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}\colon\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})\to{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}
is a canonical isomorphism of
{\mathds{Z}}
-algebras.

We propose two proofs for this result; the first is ”algebraic” while the second is of ”combinatorial” taste.
Upon completion of the first version of this paper, the author learned that Henniart and Vignéras already proved (differently) this in
[
HV15
]
.

We also show the natural generalization of Rapoport’s positivity result for unramified groups
[
Rap00
, Theorem 1.1]
, see also Haines’
[
Hai00
]
.
The key tool is due to C. Schwer
[
Sch06
, Corollary 4.8]
, in which he gives the desired positivity statement for the extended affine case.

Theorem
5.3.1

(Positivity of the twisted Satake isomorphism)
.

For any
x\in\Lambda_{M}^{-}
, write
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h_{x})=\sum_{m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-}}s_{x,m}r_{m}
. The following statements are equivalent

(1)

s_{x,m}>0
,

(2)

U^{+}m\cap KxK\neq\emptyset
,

(3)

U^{+}m\cap Iwxw^{\prime}I\neq\emptyset
, for some
w,w^{\prime}\in W_{a_{\circ}}
,

(4)

{x}\succsim{m}
, i.e.
xm^{-1}\in\mathbf{M}(F)\cap G_{1}
and
\nu_{N}(x{m}^{-1})=\sum n_{\alpha}\alpha^{\vee},n_{\alpha}\in\mathds{N}
, the sum being over the simple relative coroots.

1.1.5.
Centers of parahoric–Hecke algebras

Using normalized intertwiners in §
6.1
, we prove the following result, which is the key ingredient to describe centers of parahoric–Hecke algebras.

Theorem
6.2.1
&
6.3.1
&
6.3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}
be the fraction field of
\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\flat}:=\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
.

(1)

We have an isomorphism of
\mathcal{L}^{\flat}
-algebras:

\mathcal{L}^{\flat}*\dot{W}\to{\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}}\mathcal{H}_{{I}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
, where
\mathcal{L}^{\flat}*\dot{W}
is the skew group ring of
\dot{W}
over
\mathcal{L}^{\flat}
(See Definition
.1
)

(2)

The ring extension
\mathcal{L}^{\flat}/{\mathcal{L}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}
is
{\dot{W}}
-Galois.

(3)

The
\mathcal{L}^{\dot{W}}
-algebra
A=\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}\mathcal{H}_{{I}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
is compressible, that is
Z(eAe)=eZ(A)=e\mathcal{L}^{\dot{W}}

for any idempotent
e
of
A
.

With this result in hand, we generalize Haines’s
[
Hai09b
, Theorem 3.1.1]
which treats the case of unramified
\mathbf{G}
:

Theorem
6.4.1
.

For any facet
\mathcal{F}\subset\overline{\mathfrak{a}}
, we have

Z\left(\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})\right)=\dot{\Theta}_{\text{Bern}}^{\flat}({\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])*_{I^{\flat}}{\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}.

In particular, the set
\{r_{m}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}{\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}:m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat}\}
forms a basis for the
\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]
-module
Z\left(\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})\right)
.

I would like to point out that once the first version of this article was completed, the author learned of Haines’s paper
[
Hai14
]
.
Within the Appendix of
loc. cit.
, some general facts about the center of parahoric Hecke algebras (i.e.
M^{\flat}=M_{1}
) with
\mathds{C}
-coefficients, compatibility with constant terms and change of parahorics are treated based on the theory of Bushnell–Kutzko types.
In
loc. cit.
, Haines focuses on Iwahori levels (
\mathcal{F}=\mathfrak{a}
) and affirms the passage to general parahoric levels should rely on other properties of intertwiners for principal series representations as in
[
HKP10
]
.
However, the only written reference for these properties that the author is aware of treat only the split case
[
HKP10
]
or the unramified case
[
Hai07
]
.
The more elementary approach of the present paper treats all parahoric groups and their
\flat
-avatars directly and works with integral coefficients.

1.1.6.
Compatiblity of twisted Satake and Bernstein morphisms for parahoric levels

For any facet
\mathcal{F}\subset\overline{\mathfrak{a}}
, define the generalized twisted Satake transform
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}\colon Z(\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}))\to{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]
to be the morphism of
\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]
-algebras that is characterized by
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}(h)*_{I^{\flat}}{\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}=h
.
When
\mathcal{F}=\{a_{\circ}\}
, then
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}=\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}
.

Theorem
6.5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subset\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mathfrak{a}}
, be two facets, i.e.
K_{\mathcal{F}}\supset K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}\supset I
.
The following diagram of
\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]
-algebras is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\Theta}_{Bern}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-*_{K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}^{\flat}}{\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-*_{I^{\flat}}{\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}^{\flat}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

This commutative diagram is also compatible with change of
\flat
-avatars
M^{\flat}\subset{M^{{\flat}^{\prime}}}
(See remark
5.1.6
).
To our knowledge the first proof of the compatibility between the Satake and Bernstein isomorphism, i.e. commutativity of the diagram above is due to J.-F. Dat
[
Dat99
, §3]
for split reductive groups and for a fixed Iwahori level, i.e.
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\{a_{\circ}\}
.

1.2.
Acknowledgements

Some parts of this article originated (partially) from my doctoral thesis, directed by Dimitar Jetchev, to whom I am very grateful. I am thankful to Christophe Cornut for his support and meticulous reading, Anne-Marie Aubert and Marie-France Vigneras for many helpful comments and suggestions and Thomas Haines, for constructive advice and pointing me to his paper
[
Hai14
]
.

2.
A review of Bruhat–Tits theory

Bruhat and Tits made in their seminal work
[
BT72
,
BT84
]
a profound exploration of reductive groups over local fields by constructing for them a combinatorial avatar; for a reductive group
\mathbf{G}
over a local field
F
, they associate to
\mathbf{G}^{ad}(F)
an affine building
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
and to
\mathbf{G}(F)
a building
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{ext}
.
The first one is called the reduced Bruhat–Tits building, the latter one is called the extended Bruhat–Tits building.
Here is a gentle mise-en-bouche: the building
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
is a complete metric space, that has a structure of a poly-simplicial complex.
This building is obtained by ”gluing” a family of distinguished subsets, called apartments, these are affine spaces for some real affine space.
This inner poly-simplicial structure comes with an isometric action of the group
\mathbf{G}(F)
.

The purpose of this section is to extract a reasonably brief exposition of Bruhat–Tits theory and expose the properties that will be needed in the proofs of our results.
Moreover, most of the notations introduced for this goal will be used later.

2.1.
Notations

•

F
a finite extension of
\mathds{Q}_{p}
for some prime
p
,
\mathcal{O}_{F}
its ring of integers,
\varpi
a fixed uniformizer in
\mathcal{O}_{F}
,
\kappa(F)
its residue field and
q
its cardinality,

•

\omega\colon F^{\times}\to\mathds{Z}
the normalized discrete valuation, i.e.
\omega(\varpi)=1
and
|\cdot|_{F}=q^{-\omega(\cdot)}
for the corresponding normalized absolute value,

•

F^{sep}
a fixed separable closure,

•

For any connected reductive
F
-group
\mathbf{H}
, there exists a homomorphism of groups
\nu_{H}\colon\mathbf{H}(F)\to\text{Hom}_{\mathds{Z}}(X^{*}(\mathbf{H})_{F},\mathds{Z})
characterized by

\langle\nu_{H}(h),\chi\rangle=\nu_{H}(h)(\chi)=-\omega(\chi(h)),\text{ for all }h\in\mathbf{H}(F)\text{ and }\chi\in X^{*}(\mathbf{H})_{F}.

•

Fix
\mathbf{G}
a connected reductive group over
F
, we use the convention that all reductive groups are connected,

•

Let
\mathbf{Z}_{c}
denote the maximal central F-torus of
\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp}
its maximal
F
-split
F
-subtorus,

•

Let
\mathbf{S}
be a maximal
F
-split subtorus of
\mathbf{G}
,

•

For any algebraic subgroup
\mathbf{H}\subset\mathbf{G}
, we will denote by
N_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{H})
and
Z_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{H})
the normalizer and the centralizer of
\mathbf{H}
in
\mathbf{G}
respectively,

•

The root system of
\mathbf{G}
with respect to
\mathbf{S}
will be denoted
\Phi:=\Phi(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
,
\Phi_{red}=\{\alpha\in\Phi\colon\frac{1}{2}\alpha\notin\Phi\}
,
\Phi^{+}
a system of positive roots in
\Phi
,
\Delta
the associated base
of simple roots,
\Delta^{\vee}
its dual and the Weyl group
W=W(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})(F)=N_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{S})(F)/Z_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{S})(F)
,

•

Let
\Psi
be a closed
2
2

2

A subset
\Psi\subset\Phi_{+}\cap\Phi_{red}
such that for any
\alpha,\beta\in\Psi
one has
[\alpha,\beta]:=\{n\alpha+m\beta\colon\text{ for all }n,m\in\mathds{Z}_{>0}\}\cap\Phi\subset\Psi
.

subset of
\Phi_{red}^{+}:=\Phi^{+}\cap\Phi_{red}
, e.g.
\Psi=\{\alpha,2\alpha\}
if
2\alpha\in\Phi
. There exists a unique closed, connected, unipotent
F
-subgroup
\mathbf{U}_{\Psi}\subset\mathbf{G}
, that is normalized by
Z_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{S})
and such that the product morphism

\prod_{\alpha\in\Psi\cap\Phi_{red}}\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}\to\mathbf{U}_{\Psi},

(
\ast
)

is an isomorphism of
F
-varieties, here the product is taken in any order for
\Psi

•

Any subset
J\subset\Delta
is a base for a root system
\Phi_{J}:=\mathds{Z}J\cap\Phi
.
The subgroup
\langle Z_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{S}),\mathbf{U}_{\Phi_{J}\cup\Phi^{+}}\rangle
of
\mathbf{G}
is a standard parabolic subgroup, we denote it by
\mathbf{P}_{J}
.
We have
R_{u}(\mathbf{P}_{J})=\mathbf{U}_{\Phi^{+}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+}}
, where
\Phi_{J}^{\pm}:=\mathds{Z}J\cap\Phi^{\pm}
.

•

The Levi factor
\mathbf{M}_{J}
of
\mathbf{P}_{J}
is the subgroup generated by
\langle Z_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{S}),\mathbf{U}_{\Phi_{J}}\rangle
. Actually, this Levi subgroup is the centralizer of the split torus
\mathbf{S}_{J}:=(\cap_{\alpha\in\Phi_{J}}\ker\alpha)^{\circ}
. When
J=\emptyset
we get a minimal parabolic subgroup
\mathbf{P}_{\emptyset}
with Levi factor
Z_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{S})
. If
J=\Delta
, then
\mathbf{P}_{\Delta}=\mathbf{M}_{\Delta}=\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}
is the unique maximal split torus of the center
Z_{\mathbf{G}}
. We refer the reader to
[
Bor91
, §21]
for more details.

•

Set
\mathbf{M}:=\mathbf{M}_{\emptyset}=Z_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{S})
for the centralizer (a minimal Levi
F
-subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
),

\mathbf{N}:=N_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{S})
for the normalizer and
\mathbf{B}:=\mathbf{P}_{\emptyset}
for the minimal parabolic with Levi factor
\mathbf{M}
and unipotent radical
\mathbf{U}^{+}=\mathbf{U}_{\Phi^{+}}
, we have
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{U}_{\Phi^{+}}\rtimes\mathbf{M}
.

•

We will sometimes use the notation
\square\otimes k=\square_{k}
for a field
k
.

2.2.
The standard apartment

We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{*}(\mathbf{M})_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{*}(\mathbf{M})_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{*}(\mathbf{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{*}(\mathbf{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{Z}}

where only the bottom line is a perfect pairing.
In the following lemma we will show that, the injective finite-cokernel group homomorphism
X^{*}(\mathbf{M})_{F}\hookrightarrow X^{*}(\mathbf{S})
induces a unique homomorphism
\mathbf{M}(F)\to X_{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}
that extend
\nu_{M}\colon\mathbf{M}(F)\to\text{Hom}_{\mathds{Z}}(X^{*}(\mathbf{M})_{F},\mathds{Z})
.

Lemma 2.2.1

There exists a unique homomorphism
\nu_{M}\colon\mathbf{M}(F)\to X_{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}
such that

\langle\nu_{M}(z),\chi|_{\mathbf{S}}\rangle=-\omega(\chi(z)),\text{ for all }z\in\mathbf{M}(F)\text{ and }\chi\in X^{*}(\mathbf{M})_{F}(\hookrightarrow X^{*}(\mathbf{S})).

Proof.

Because the restriction map
X^{*}(\mathbf{M})_{F}\to X^{*}(\mathbf{S})
is injective and has an image of finite index, i.e. for any
\chi\in X^{*}(\mathbf{S})
, the character
[X^{*}(\mathbf{S}):X^{*}(\mathbf{M})_{F}]\chi\in X^{*}(\mathbf{S})
extends to a unique
\widetilde{\chi}\in X^{*}(\mathbf{M})_{F}
.
Consider the following map

M(F)\to\text{Hom}(X^{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q},\mathds{Q}),\quad z\mapsto\left(\chi\otimes r\mapsto-r\frac{1}{[X^{*}(\mathbf{S}):X^{*}(\mathbf{M})_{F}]}\omega(\widetilde{\chi}(z))\right).

This is clearly a homomorphism of groups.
Moreover,
X^{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}
and
X_{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}
remain a dual pair of
\mathds{Q}
-modules for the canonical pairing
\langle\,,\rangle_{\mathds{Q}}
that extend the pairing
\langle\,,\rangle\colon X_{*}(\mathbf{S})\times X^{*}(\mathbf{S})\to\mathds{Z}
.
Thus, any homomorphism in
\text{Hom}(X^{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q},\mathds{Q})
is of the form
\chi\otimes r\mapsto\langle\chi,\lambda\rangle rr^{\prime}
for some
\lambda\otimes r^{\prime}\in X_{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}
.
This defines a unique homomorphism of groups
\nu_{M}\colon\mathbf{M}(F)\to X^{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}
verifying

\langle\nu_{M}(z),\chi\otimes 1\rangle_{\mathds{Q}}=-\frac{1}{[X^{*}(\mathbf{S}):X^{*}(\mathbf{M})_{F}]}\omega(\widetilde{\chi}(z))\quad\text{for all }z\in\mathbf{M}(F)\text{ and }\chi\in X^{*}(\mathbf{S}),

or equivalently (since
X^{*}(\mathbf{M})_{F}
generates
X^{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}
)

\displaystyle\langle\nu_{M}(z),\chi_{|_{\mathbf{S}}}\otimes 1\rangle_{\mathds{Q}}

\displaystyle=-\omega({\chi}(z))\quad\text{for all }z\in\mathbf{M}(F)\text{ and }\chi\in X^{*}(\mathbf{M})_{F}.\qed

By
[
Lan96
, Proposition 1.2]
, the subgroup
\mathbf{M}(F)^{1}:=\ker\nu_{M}
is the maximal compact open subgroup of
\mathbf{M}(F)
.
We then have a short exact sequence of groups

0\to\mathbf{M}(F)/\mathbf{M}(F)^{1}\to\mathbf{N}(F)/\mathbf{M}(F)^{1}\to W=\mathbf{N}(F)/\mathbf{M}(F)\to 0,

in which
\mathbf{M}(F)/\mathbf{M}(F)^{1}
is a free abelian group containing
X_{*}(\mathbf{S})
and having same rank
[
Lan96
, Lemma 1.3]
.

Consider the
\mathds{R}
-vector space
V=X_{*}(\mathbf{S}/\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{R}
and identify its dual space
V^{*}
with
X^{*}(\mathbf{S}/\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{R}
using the canonical extended pairing
\langle\,,\rangle_{\mathds{R}}
.
We denote by

\nu\colon\mathbf{M}(F)\to V,

the composition of the map
\nu_{M}\colon\mathbf{M}(F)\rightarrow X_{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{R}
with the natural projection
X_{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{R}\twoheadrightarrow V
.

The action of
W
on
\mathbf{S}
by conjugation induces a faithful linear action on
X_{*}(\mathbf{S})
, which induces a canonical homomorphism of groups

j\colon W\to GL(X_{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{R}),

and accordingly a homomorphism
W\to GL(V)
since
\mathbf{N}(F)
acts trivially on
\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp}
.

Proposition 2.2.2

There is a canonical affine space
\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
under
V
(unique up to a unique isomorphism of affine spaces) together with a group homomorphism
\nu_{N}\colon\mathbf{N}(F)\to\textit{\emph{\text{Aff}}}(\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S}))
extending
\nu
. Accordingly, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{M}(F)/\mathbf{M}(F)^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{N}(F)/\mathbf{M}(F)^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textit{\emph{\text{Aff}}}(\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GL(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

For all
z\in\mathbf{M}(F)
,
\nu_{N}(z)
is the translation
a\mapsto\nu(z)+a
and for any
n\in\mathbf{N}(F)
the linear part of
\nu_{N}(n)
is equal to
j(\jmath_{W}(n))
, where
\jmath_{W}(n)
denotes the image of
n
in
W
.

Proof.

See the proof of
[
Lan96
, Proposition 1.8]
.
∎

Remark 2.2.3
.

(i) The kernel
\ker\nu_{N}
contains
\mathbf{M}(F)^{1}
and the center of
\mathbf{G}(F)
.
(ii) The reductivity of
\mathbf{G}
forces the action of the Weyl group
W
on
V_{0}=X_{*}(Z_{\mathbf{G}})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{R}
to be trivial, which implies the injectivity of the morphism
W\hookrightarrow GL(V)
and accordingly

\ker\nu_{N}=\ker\nu
.
(iii) All possible extensions of
\nu
are of the form

\nu_{N,v}(n)\colon a\mapsto-v+\nu_{N}(n)(v+a),\,\forall a\in\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S}),

for some fixed
v\in V
.

We are now ready to define the central combinatorial object of this section

Definition 2.2.4
.

The affine space
\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
together with the group homomorphism
\nu_{N}\colon\mathbf{N}(F)\to\textit{\emph{\text{Aff}}}(\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S}))
is called the standard apartment of
\mathbf{G}
with respect to
\mathbf{S}
.

2.3.
A discrete valuation of the generating root datum

Define for each
\alpha\in\Phi
the set
M_{\alpha}
of elements
n\in\mathbf{N}(F)
whose image in the Weyl group
\jmath_{W}(n)\in W
is the reflection
s_{\alpha}\colon V\to V,v\mapsto v-\langle v,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\alpha
, where (by abuse of notation)
\alpha^{\vee}
denotes the image of the co-root in
V
.

A good part of the theory developed by Borel and Tits in
[
BT65
]
(see
[
BT72
, §6.1]
) may be condensed in the following fundamental theorem:

Theorem 2.3.1

The datum
\left(\mathbf{M}(F),\left(\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}(F),M_{\alpha}\right)_{\alpha\in\Phi}\right)
is a generating root datum
of type
\Phi
in
\mathbf{G}(F)
.

The nomenclature ”generating root datum” means that the above datum verifies the list of axioms
[
BT72
, §6.1.1]
.

Corollary 2.3.2

For any
\alpha\in\Phi
and any
u\in\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}(F)\setminus\{1\}
there exists a unique pair

(u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime})\in{\mathbf{U}_{-\alpha}(F)\setminus\{1\}\times\mathbf{U}_{-\alpha}(F)\setminus\{1\}}

such that
m_{\alpha}(u):=u^{\prime}uu^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbf{N}(F)
and verifies
m_{\alpha}(u)\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}(F)m_{\alpha}(u)^{-1}=\mathbf{U}_{-\alpha}(F)
.

In particular,
\mathbf{U}_{-\alpha}(F)u\mathbf{U}_{-\alpha}(F)\cap\mathbf{N}(F)=\{m_{\alpha}(u)\}
.
Moreover,
m_{\alpha}(u)
normalizes
\mathbf{S}
and its image in
W
acts on
V
by the reflexion
s_{\alpha}
.
Accordingly, the set
M_{\alpha}
defined above Theorem
2.3.1
is precisely the right coset
m_{\alpha}(u)\mathbf{M}(F)
.

Proof.

See
[
BT72
, §6.1.2]
.
∎

Fix any point (origin)
a_{\circ}
in
\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
. For all
\alpha\in\Phi
and all
u\in\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}(F)\setminus\{1\}
we have

\nu_{N}(m_{\alpha}(u))(a)=s_{\alpha}(a-a_{\circ})+\nu_{N}(m_{\alpha}(u))(a_{\circ})=-\alpha(a-a_{\circ})\alpha^{\vee}+a-a_{\circ}+\nu_{N}(m_{\alpha}(u))(a_{\circ})

The set of fixed points of
\nu_{N}(m_{\alpha}(u))
is an affine hyperplane in
\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
with direction
\ker(s_{\alpha}-\text{Id}_{V})
. Let
b_{a_{\circ},\alpha,u}
be any fixed element of this hyperplane, one has

\nu_{N}(m_{\alpha}(u))(a_{\circ})=\alpha(b_{a_{\circ},\alpha,u}-a_{\circ})\alpha^{\vee}+a_{\circ}.

Therefore,

\nu_{N}(m_{\alpha}(u))(a)=s_{\alpha}(a-a_{\circ})+\alpha(b_{a_{\circ},\alpha,u}-a_{\circ})\alpha^{\vee}+a_{\circ}.

Now, by setting
\varphi_{\alpha}^{a_{\circ}}(u):=-\alpha(b_{a_{\circ},\alpha,u}-a_{\circ})\in\mathds{R}
one can rewrite
\nu_{N}(m_{\alpha}(u))
as follows:

\nu_{N}(m_{\alpha}(u))(a)=-\left(\alpha(a-a_{\circ})+\varphi_{\alpha}^{a_{\circ}}(u)\right)\alpha^{\vee}+a.

Accordingly, the element
\nu_{N}(m_{\alpha}(u))
acts as a reflection with respect to the hyperplane
\{a\in\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})\colon\alpha(a-a_{\circ})=-\varphi_{\alpha}^{a_{\circ}}(u)\}
(See
[
BT72
, Remark (b) 6.2.12]
).

One of the fundamental results obtained by Bruhat and Tits is

Theorem 2.3.3

The family
\varphi^{a_{\circ}}=(\varphi_{\alpha}^{a_{\circ}}\colon\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}(F)\to\mathds{R}\cup\{\infty\})_{\alpha\in\Phi}
is a discrete valuation of the generating root datum
\left(\mathbf{M}(F),\left(\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}(F),M_{\alpha}\right)_{\alpha\in\Phi}\right)
,
meaning that
\varphi^{a_{\circ}}
verifies the properties listed in
[
BT72
, §6.2.1 & §6.2.21]
.

Proof.

For a proof we refer the reader to
[
BT84
, §5.1.20 & §5.1.23]
and
[
BT72
, Remarque 6.2.12 (b)]
.∎

Consequently, for all
\alpha\in\Phi
and
r\in\mathds{R}\cup\{\infty\}
, the set
U_{\alpha+r}^{a_{\circ}}:=(\varphi_{\alpha}^{a_{\circ}})^{-1}([r,\infty])
is a compact open subgroup of
\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}(F)
and
U_{\alpha+\infty}^{a_{\circ}}=\{1\}
. Moreover, if
2\alpha\in\Phi
then
\varphi_{2\alpha}^{a_{\circ}}
is the restriction of
2\varphi_{\alpha}^{a_{\circ}}
to
U_{2\alpha}
.

For any
v\in V
, the map
a_{\circ}\mapsto v+a_{\circ}
yields the map

\varphi^{a_{\circ}}\mapsto v+\varphi^{a_{\circ}}:=\varphi^{v+a_{\circ}}=\left(\varphi^{v+a_{\circ}}_{\alpha}\colon\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}(F)\to\mathds{R}\cup\{\infty\},u\mapsto\varphi^{a_{\circ}}(u)+\alpha(v)\right)_{\alpha\in\Phi}.

Two discrete valuations in
\mathds{A}_{\varphi^{a_{\circ}}}:=\{v+\varphi^{a_{\circ}}\colon v\in V\}
are called equipollent. As noted by
[
BT72
, 6.2.6]
, the action of
V
on
\mathds{A}_{\varphi^{a_{\circ}}}
described above endow this latter with a structure of an affine space under
V
.

Remark 2.3.4
.

Under the isomorphism of affine spaces
\mathds{A}_{\varphi^{a_{\circ}}}\simeq\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
, the action of
N
on
\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
described in Proposition
2.2.2
corresponds to the following action of
N
on
\mathds{A}_{\varphi^{a_{\circ}}}
:

N\times\mathds{A}_{\varphi^{a_{\circ}}}\to\mathds{A}_{\varphi^{a_{\circ}}},\quad[n,v+\varphi^{a_{\circ}}]\mapsto\jmath_{W}(n)(v)+\varphi^{\nu_{N}(n)(a_{\circ})}.

For any
\alpha\in\Phi
, the valuation
\varphi^{a_{\circ}}
is discrete;
\Gamma_{\alpha}(a_{\circ}):=\varphi_{\alpha}^{a_{\circ}}(\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}(F)\setminus\{1\})
is a discrete subset of
\mathds{R}

[
BT72
, §6.2.21]
and
\Gamma_{\alpha}(a_{\circ})=\Gamma_{-\alpha}(a_{\circ})
.
Set

\Gamma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(a_{\circ}):=\{\varphi_{\alpha}^{a_{\circ}}(u)\in\Gamma_{\alpha}(a_{\circ})\colon\varphi_{\alpha}^{a_{\circ}}(u)=\sup\varphi_{\alpha}^{a_{\circ}}(u\mathbf{U}_{2\alpha}(F))\}

(If
2\alpha\not\in\Phi
we put
U_{2\alpha}=\{1\}
, i.e.
\Gamma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(a_{\circ})=\Gamma_{\alpha}(a_{\circ})
).
We have

\Gamma_{\alpha}(a_{\circ})=\Gamma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(a_{\circ})\cup\frac{1}{2}\Gamma_{2\alpha}(a_{\circ})

[
BT72
, §6.2.2]
.

Remark 2.3.5
.

The set
\Gamma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(a_{\circ})
can be empty, in our discrete case, this happens if and only if
U_{2\alpha}
is dense in
U_{\alpha}

[
BT72
, §6.2.2]
.

Definition 2.3.6
.

We say that a discrete valuation

\varphi^{a}\in\mathds{A}_{\varphi^{a_{\circ}}}
(for some
a\in\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
) is special if, for any
\alpha\in\Phi_{red}
, one has
0\in\Gamma_{\alpha}(a)
.

Remark 2.3.7
.

By definition
\varphi^{a}=(\varphi_{\alpha}^{a}\colon u\mapsto-\alpha(b_{a,\alpha,u}-a))
, we see then that
\varphi^{a}
is special if and only if for any
\alpha\in\Phi_{red}
, there is a
u_{\alpha}\in\mathbf{U}(F)
such that
a=b_{a,\alpha,u_{\alpha}}
, this is equivalent to saying that
a
is fixed by
m_{\alpha}(u_{\alpha})
for all
\alpha\in\Phi_{red}
, in which case we will say that
a
is a special point of
\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
.

Lemma 2.3.8

There exists a special discrete valuation in
\mathds{A}_{\varphi^{a_{\circ}}}
.

Proof.

This is
[
BT72
, Corollaire 6.2.15]
.∎

By the previous lemma we may and will assume from now on that
a_{\circ}
is a special point. From now on we will omit indicating
a_{\circ}
in the notation of
\varphi^{a_{\circ}},\Gamma_{\alpha}(a_{\circ}),\Gamma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(a_{\circ}),\varphi_{\alpha}^{a_{\circ}}
and
U_{\alpha+r}^{a_{\circ}}
for
\alpha\in\Phi,r\in\mathds{R}
, i.e. we will write
\varphi,\varphi_{\alpha}
and
U_{\alpha+r}
…

Proposition 2.3.9

For any
\alpha\in\Phi
, there exists a positive integer
n_{\alpha}
such that
\Gamma_{\alpha}=n_{\alpha}^{-1}\mathds{Z}
, which satisfies the following properties:

n_{\alpha}=n_{-\alpha}
,
n_{w(\alpha)}=n_{\alpha}
for any
w\in W=W(\Phi)

and
n_{\alpha}
is divisible by
n_{2\alpha}
if
\alpha,2\alpha\in\Phi
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
SS97
, Lemma I.2.10]
and
[
BT72
, 6.2.23]
.
∎

2.4.
Affine roots

In the notation
U_{\alpha+r}
for any
\alpha\in\Phi
and any
r\in\Gamma_{\alpha}
,
\alpha+r
should be interpreted as the closed half-space of
\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
, on which the affine mapping
a\mapsto\alpha(a-a_{\circ})+r
is positive.

Definition 2.4.1
.

Let
\Phi_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
denote the set of all affine roots
\left\{\alpha+r\colon\alpha\in\Phi,r\in\Gamma_{\alpha}^{\prime}\right\}

[
BT72
, 6.2.6]
.

For use later, we describe in the following lemma the action of
\mathbf{N}(F)
on root groups
U_{\alpha+r}
for affine roots.

Lemma 2.4.2

Let
n\in\mathbf{N}(F)
, with image
w
in
W
.
We have for all
\alpha\in\Phi
and all
r\in\Gamma_{\alpha}^{\prime}
:

nU_{\alpha+r}n^{-1}=U_{\beta},

where,
\beta=w(\alpha)+r-w(\alpha)(\nu_{N}(n)(a_{\circ})-a_{\circ})
. In particular,
mU_{\alpha+r}m^{-1}=U_{\alpha+r-\langle\nu(m),\alpha\rangle}
for
m\in\mathbf{M}(F)
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha+r\in\Phi_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
and
n\in N
of image
w
in
W
. As suggested by the proof of
[
BT72
, 6.2.10. (iii)]
:

\displaystyle nU_{\alpha+r}n^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\varphi_{\alpha})^{-1}([r,\infty])

\displaystyle=n\{u\in\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}(F)\colon\varphi_{\alpha}(u)\geq r\}n^{-1}

\displaystyle=\{u\in\mathbf{U}_{w(\alpha)}(F)\colon\varphi_{\alpha}(n^{-1}un)\geq r\}

\displaystyle=\{u\in\mathbf{U}_{w(\alpha)}(F)\colon(\nu_{N}(\varphi))_{w(\alpha)}(u)\geq r\}

\displaystyle{=}\{u\in\mathbf{U}_{w(\alpha)}(F)\colon(w(0_{V})+\varphi^{\nu_{N}(a_{\circ})})_{w(\alpha)}(u)\geq r\}

\displaystyle=\{u\in\mathbf{U}_{w(\alpha)}(F)\colon(\varphi^{\nu_{N}(a_{\circ})})_{w(\alpha)}(u)\geq r\}

\displaystyle{=}\{u\in\mathbf{U}_{w(\alpha)}(F)\colon(\nu_{N}(n)(a_{\circ})-a_{\circ}+\varphi)_{w(\alpha)}(u)\geq r\}

\displaystyle=\{u\in\mathbf{U}_{w(\alpha)}(F)\colon\varphi_{w(\alpha)}(u)+w(\alpha)(\nu_{N}(n)(a_{\circ})-a_{\circ})\geq r\}

\displaystyle=U_{\beta=w(\alpha)+r-w(\alpha)(\nu_{N}(n)(a_{\circ})-a_{\circ})}\qed

For each affine root
\alpha+r\in\Phi_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
consider the affine hyperplane
H_{\alpha+r}:=\big\{a\in\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})\colon\alpha(a-a_{\circ})=-r\}\big\}
.
For any
u_{r}\in\varphi_{\alpha}^{-1}(r)
, one has
H_{\alpha+r}=\{a\in\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})\colon m_{\alpha}(u_{r})(a)=a\}
.
If
\alpha,2\alpha\in\Phi
, then for any
r\in\Gamma_{2\alpha}
, we have
H_{2\alpha+r}=H_{\alpha+r/2}
.
These hyperplanes define a poly-simplicial structure on the standard apartment in the following way:

Definition 2.4.3
.

Define the equivalence relation on
\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
by
a\sim b
if for any affine root
\beta=\alpha+r
the sign of
\alpha(a-a_{\circ})+r
and
\alpha(b-a_{\circ})+r
is the same or are both equal to zero;
3
3

3

Equivalently,
a\sim b
if for any affine hyperplane
H
, either
a,b\in H
or they are in the same connected component of
\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})\setminus H
.

the equivalence classes are called the facets.
A vertex is a point which is a facet, e.g. the point
a_{\circ}
.
A facet with maximal dimension is called an alcove, it is also a connected component
\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})\setminus\bigcup_{\beta\in\Phi_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}}H_{\beta}
.

From now on, we fix an alcove
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
containing in its closure the special vertex
a_{\circ}
.

Definition 2.4.4
.

Let
\alpha\in\Phi
and
r\in\Gamma_{\alpha}
. We say that
\alpha+r
is
\mathfrak{a}
-positive (resp.
\mathfrak{a}
-negative) if
\alpha(a-a_{\circ})+r>0
(resp.
<0
) for some
a\in\mathfrak{a}
(then for all, since the sign does not depend on the choice of
a\in\mathfrak{a}
). Let
\Phi_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{+}
(resp.
\Phi_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{-}
) denote the set of affine
\mathfrak{a}
-positive (resp. negative) affine roots.

For any non-empty subset
\Upomega\subset\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
and
\alpha\in\Phi
, we define
f_{\Upomega}\colon\Phi\to\mathds{R}\cup\{\infty\}
,

f_{\Upomega}(\alpha):=\inf\{r\in\Gamma_{\alpha}\colon(\alpha+r)(\Upomega)\subset\mathds{R}^{+}\}.

Define also the group
U_{\Upomega}
to be the subgroup of
\mathbf{G}(F)
generated by
\cup_{\alpha\in\Phi_{red}}U_{\alpha+f_{\Upomega}(\alpha)}
.
Note that for
a\in\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
and
\alpha\in\Phi
, the real
f_{\{a\}}(\alpha)
depends only on the facet containing
a
.

Proposition 2.4.5

For any non-empty subset
\Upomega\subset\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
, the groups defined above have the following important properties:

(1)

For any
n\in\mathbf{N}(F)
we have
nU_{\Upomega}n^{-1}=U_{\nu_{N}(n)\cdot\Upomega}
, so in particular
N_{\Upomega}:=\{n\in\mathbf{N}(F)\colon\nu_{N}(n)\cdot x=x\text{ for all }x\in\Upomega\}
normalizes
U_{\Upomega}
.

(2)

For any
\alpha\in\Phi_{red}
we have
U_{\Upomega}\cap U_{\alpha}=U_{\alpha+f_{\Upomega}(\alpha)}
.

(3)

The set
P_{\Upomega}:=N_{\Upomega}U_{\Upomega}=U_{\Upomega}N_{\Upomega}
is a group.
We have
P_{\Upomega}\cap\mathbf{N}(F)=N_{\Upomega}
.

For any decomposition into positive and negative roots
\Phi=\Phi^{+}\sqcup\Phi^{-}
, we have

(4)

The following product map is an

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi_{red}^{\pm}}U_{\alpha+f_{\Upomega}(\alpha)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\Upomega}\cap\mathbf{U}(F)^{\pm}=:U_{\Upomega}^{\pm}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

homeomorphism whatever ordering of the factors we take.

(5)

U_{\Upomega}=U_{\Upomega}^{+}U_{\Upomega}^{-}(U_{\Upomega}\cap\mathbf{N}(F))
.

Proof.

See
[
BT72
, 6.2.10(iii),6.4.9, 7.1.3& 7.1.8]
.
We warn the reader that the groups denoted here
N_{\Upomega}
and
N_{\Upomega}
are denoted by
\widehat{N}_{\Upomega}
and
\widehat{P}_{\Upomega}
.
∎

From now, we will adopt the following notation: when
\Upomega=\{x\}
we will write
\square_{x}
instead of
\square_{\{x\}}
for
\square\in\{f,U,N,P\}
.

Example 2.4.6
.

1 - For
\Upomega=\{a_{\circ}\}
, we have
f_{\{a_{\circ}\}}(\alpha)=0
for all
\alpha\in\Phi
. Now, let us see the case
\Upomega=\mathfrak{a}
.
We first have

\mathfrak{a}=\{a\in\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})\colon 0<\alpha(a-a_{\circ})<n_{\alpha}^{-1}\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Phi\text{ that are $\mathfrak{a}$-positive}\}.

Therefore, if
\alpha\in\Phi
is
\mathfrak{a}
-positive, then
f_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha)=0
.
If now
\alpha\in\Phi_{red}
is
\mathfrak{a}
-negative, then
-\alpha
is
\mathfrak{a}
-positive, hence for all
a\in\mathfrak{a}
we have
0<-\alpha(a-a_{\circ})<n_{-\alpha}^{-1}
. The real
f_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha)\in\Gamma_{\alpha}
being the smallest element such that
\alpha(a-a_{\circ})\geq-f_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha)
, we see that
f_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha)=n_{-\alpha}^{-1}
.
We know that
\Gamma_{\alpha}=\Gamma_{-\alpha}
, this implies that
n_{\alpha}^{-1}=n_{-\alpha}^{-1}
.
In conclusion:

f_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha)=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if $\alpha\in\Phi$ is $\mathfrak{a}$-positive},\\
n_{\alpha}^{-1}&\text{ if $\alpha\in\Phi$ is $\mathfrak{a}$-negative}\end{cases}

2 - If
\Upomega_{1}\subset\Upomega_{2}
, then
f_{\Upomega_{1}}(\alpha)\geq f_{\Upomega_{2}}(\alpha)
, for all
\alpha\in\Phi
. For example, if
\mathcal{F}
is facet of
\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
such that
a_{\circ}\in\overline{\mathcal{F}}\subset\overline{\mathfrak{a}}
, then

f_{\mathcal{F}}(\alpha)=0\text{ if $\alpha\in\Phi$ is $\mathfrak{a}$-positive},\text{ and }f_{\mathcal{F}}(\alpha)\in\{0,n_{\alpha}^{-1}\}\text{ if $\alpha\in\Phi$ is $\mathfrak{a}$-negative}.

3 - For any non-empty subset
\Upomega\subset\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
and any
n\in\widetilde{W}
whose image in
W
is
w
, we have by Lemma
2.4.2

f_{\nu_{N}(n)\cdot\Upomega}(\alpha)=w(\alpha)(\nu_{N}(n)(a_{\circ})-a_{\circ})+f_{\Upomega}(w^{-1}(\alpha)).

2.5.
Affine Weyl groups

For any affine root
\beta=\alpha+r\in\Phi_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
, let
s_{\beta}\in\nu_{N}(\mathbf{N}(F))
denote the orthogonal reflection with respect to the hyperplane
H_{\beta}
:

s_{\beta}\colon a\mapsto-\left(\alpha(a-a_{\circ})+r\right)\alpha^{\vee}+a,\,\forall a\in\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S}).

Definition 2.5.1
.

We define the affine Weyl group
W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}\subset\nu_{N}(\mathbf{N}(F))
to be the group generated by the reflections
s_{\beta}
for all
\beta\in\Phi_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
. It is a normal subgroup of
\nu_{N}(\mathbf{N}(F))

[
BT72
, 6.2.11]
.

By
[
BT84
, §4.2.21 & §5.1.19]
, we have
\Phi^{\prime}:=\{\alpha\in\Phi\colon\Gamma_{\alpha}^{\prime}\neq 0\}=\Phi
.
The set
\Sigma:=\{n_{\alpha}\alpha\colon\alpha\in\Phi\}
is a root system and the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{red,\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}:=\cup_{\alpha\in\Phi_{red}}(\alpha+\Gamma_{\alpha}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}:=\cup_{\alpha\in\Sigma}(\alpha+\mathds{Z});\quad(\alpha+r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{\alpha}(\alpha+r),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

is bijective and respects positivity.
The group
W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}=W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}(\Sigma)
is the affine Weyl group associated with
\Sigma
and
\Phi_{red,\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
is the corresponding affine roots system

[
BT72
, 6.2.22]
.
This is also the normal subgroup of
\nu_{N}(\mathbf{N}(F))
generated by the set of orthogonal reflections
s_{\alpha+r}
for all
\alpha\in\Phi
and all
r\in\Gamma_{\alpha}
.

Define
\Lambda_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
to be the subgroup of translations in
W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
, we can then identify
\Lambda_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
with the coroot lattice
\mathds{Z}[\Sigma^{\vee}]

[
BT72
, Proposition 6.2.20]
.
The affine Weyl group
W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
acts simply transitively on the set of alcoves in
\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
(VI §2.1
loc. cit.
).

Let
a
be vertex in
\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
, we denote by
\Phi_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{a}
the set of affine roots that vanish at
a
, set
W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{a}=\langle s_{\beta}\colon\beta\in\Phi_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{a}\rangle\subset W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
. The vertex
a
is special in the sense of Remark
2.3.7
if and only if the composition of the following maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{N}(\mathbf{N}(F))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W,}

is an isomorphism
4
4

4

This is equivalent to
W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{a}\simeq W
or just to
\#W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{a}=\#W
since the induced map
W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{a}\hookrightarrow W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}\to W
is always injective.
. Special vertices exists by
[
BT72
, Corollaire 6.2.16]
.
We have then by
[
BT72
, 1.3 & 6.2.19]
a decomposition for the affine Weyl group

W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}\simeq\Lambda_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}\rtimes W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{a}\simeq\mathds{Z}[\Sigma^{\vee}]\rtimes W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{a},

for any fixed special vertex
a\in\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
.

Definition 2.5.2
.

A face (resp. facet) of the alcove
\mathfrak{a}
is a facet contained in a single affine hyperplane (resp. in
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}
).
A wall of the alcove
\mathfrak{a}
is a hyperplane containing a face of
\mathfrak{a}
.
Set

S(\mathfrak{a}):=\{s_{H}\colon\text{$H$ a wall of $\mathfrak{a}$}\}.

Define the type of a facet
\mathcal{F}
of
\mathfrak{a}
to be the set

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}=\{s_{H}\colon\text{$H$ a wall of $\mathfrak{a}$, }\mathcal{F}\subset H\}\subset S(\mathfrak{a}).

Let
W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\mathcal{F}}
be the group generated by
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}
. Then
(W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\mathcal{F}},\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}})
is a finite Coxeter system.

Remark 2.5.3
.

A facet is determined by its type

\mathcal{F}=\{a\in\overline{\mathfrak{a}}\colon(a\in H\Longleftrightarrow\mathcal{F}\subset H)\text{ for any wall $H$ of $\mathfrak{a}$}.\}

Since a facet of
\mathcal{F}
is the image by an element of
W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
of a unique facet of
\mathfrak{a}
and each facet of
\mathfrak{a}
is determined by its type, one can then define the type for all facets
[
BT72
, 1.3.5]
. So alcoves have empty types (
\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{a}}=\emptyset
) and special points have full type and
W_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{a_{\circ}}
is generated by
\mathcal{T}_{a_{\circ}}
.

2.6.
The reduced Bruhat–Tits building

We arrive now at the most important object of this section, that is the reduced Bruhat–Tits building:

Definition 2.6.1
.

Define the set

\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}:=\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})/\sim,

where
\sim
is the equivalence relation on
\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
defined by

(g,x)\sim(h,y)\,\text{ if }\exists n\in\mathbf{N}(F)\text{ such that }nx=y\text{ and }g^{-1}hn\in U_{x}.

The group
\mathbf{G}(F)
acts on
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
on the left:

g\cdot[(h,y)]:=[(gh,y)]\quad\text{ for }g\in\mathbf{G}(F)\text{ and }(h,y)\in\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S}).

Moreover, the map
\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})\to\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
given by
x\mapsto[(1,x)]
is an
\mathbf{N}(F)
-equivariant embedding. We will denote its image by
\mathcal{A}
. We have
g\cdot\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}
(resp.
g\cdot x=x,\,\forall x\in\mathcal{A}
) if and only if
g\in\mathbf{N}(F)
(resp.
g\in\ker\nu_{N}
)
[
BT72
, 7.4.10]
.

Definition 2.6.2
.

An apartment of the building
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
is a subset of the form
g\mathcal{A}
for some
g\in\mathbf{G}(F)
. A facet (resp. alcove) of
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
is a subset of the form
g\mathcal{F}
for some
g\in\mathbf{G}(F)
and a facet (resp. alcove)
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{A}
.

Since all maximal
F
-split
F
-tori of
\mathbf{G}
are
\mathbf{G}(F)
-conjugate
[
Bor91
, Theorem 20.9]
,
all apartments of
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
are in bijection with maximal split tori of
\mathbf{G}
.

Proposition 2.6.3

In this proposition, we will collect some important properties regarding the building
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
:

(1)

(Fixators) Let
\Upomega\subset\mathcal{A}
. We have an alternative characterization for the subgroups
P_{\Upomega}=N_{\Upomega}U_{\Upomega}
(defined in Proposition
2.4.5
-
3
):

P_{\Upomega}=\text{Fix}(\mathbf{G}(F),\Upomega):=\{g\in\mathbf{G}(F)\colon gx=x\quad\forall x\in\Upomega\},

in other words
P_{\Upomega}
is the subgroup that fixes all points of
\Upomega

[
BT72
, 7.4.4]
.

(2)

For any
g\in\mathbf{G}(F)
we can find a
n\in\mathbf{N}(F)
verifying
gx=nx
for all
x
in the closed subset
\mathcal{A}\cap g^{-1}\mathcal{A}

[
BT72
, 7.4.8]
.

(3)

(Transitivity) Let
\Upomega\subset\mathcal{A}
. The group
U_{\Upomega}
acts transitively on the set of all apartments containing
\Upomega

[
BT72
, 7.4.9]
.

(4)

For any two facets in the building, there exists an apartment that contains both of them
[
BT72
, 7.4.18]
.

(5)

Let us fix an
W
-invariant euclidean metric
d
on
\mathcal{A}
. The previous properties ensure that the distance
d
extends uniquely to a
\mathbf{G}(F)
-invariant metric on the set
(\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red},d)
.

Remark 2.6.4
.

The term ”fixators” refers to pointwise stabilizers, in contrast the term ”stabilizers” will be used for setwise stabilizers.∎

Definition 2.6.5
.

The reduced Bruhat–Tits building of
\mathbf{G}(F)
is the pair
(\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red},d)
together with its isometric
\mathbf{G}(F)
-action and the poly-simplicial structure defined by its facets.

2.7.
The extended Bruhat–Tits building

2.7.1.
The extended apartment

When the center
Z(\mathbf{G})
has split rank
>0
, the stabilizer
P_{x}
of a point
x\in\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
is no longer a compact subgroup of
\mathbf{G}(F)
. To remedy this ”issue” we define in this section a larger building following
[
BT84
, 4.2.16]
.

We have a decomposition
X_{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}=(X_{*}(\mathbf{S}^{der})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q})\oplus(X_{*}(\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q})
,
where
\mathbf{S}^{der}=(\mathbf{G}^{der}\cap\mathbf{S})^{\circ}
.
This is the ”dual” statement of the decomposition (2) in Lemma
[
Con19
, Lemma 11.3.3]
.
This decomposition allows us to inject
\Phi^{\vee}
in
V^{\vee}
. So, the ”dual” statement of (3) in
loc. cit.
together with
[
Bor91
, Theorem 201.6 & Remark 21.1]
imply that
V=\text{span}_{\mathds{R}}(\Phi^{\vee})=X_{*}(\mathbf{S}^{der})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{R}
.
Accordingly, the apartment
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
corresponding to
\mathbf{S}
can be viewd as a torsor for the real vector space

X_{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{R}\big/X_{*}(\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{R}.

Let us construct a homomorphism
\nu_{G}\colon\mathbf{G}(F)\to X_{*}(\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}
:
Using the isogeny
\mathbf{Z}_{c}\times\mathbf{G}^{der}\to\mathbf{G}
, we see that

X^{*}(\mathbf{G})_{F}=X^{*}(\mathbf{G}/\mathbf{G}^{der})_{F}
. Since the quotient map
\mathbf{Z}_{c}\to\mathbf{G}/\mathbf{G}^{der}
is an
F
-isogeny of
F
-tori, we see that
X^{*}(\mathbf{G}/\mathbf{G}^{der})_{F}
identifies with a subgroup of
X^{*}(\mathbf{Z}_{c})_{F}
of finite index. Therefore, we have a natural isomorphism of
\mathds{Q}
-vector spaces

X^{*}(\mathbf{G}/\mathbf{G}^{der})_{F}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}\to X^{*}(\mathbf{Z}_{c})_{F}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}.

Identify the
\mathds{Q}
-linear dual
\text{Hom}_{\mathds{Z}}(X^{*}(\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp}),\mathds{Q})
of
X^{*}(\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}
with
X_{*}(\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}
. In conclusion, we get a natural isomorphism

X^{*}(\mathbf{G})_{F}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}\simeq X^{*}(\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}.

Consequently, we obtain a canonical isomorphism

X_{*}(\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}\simeq\text{Hom}_{\mathds{Z}}(X^{*}(\mathbf{G})_{F}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q},\mathds{Q}).

This shows that there exists a unique homomorphism

\mathbf{G}(F)\to X_{*}(\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q},

extending
\nu_{G}\colon\mathbf{G}(F)\to\text{Hom}_{\mathds{Z}}(X^{*}(\mathbf{G})_{F},\mathds{Z})
(§
2.1
); this is the unique homomorphism denoted by abuse of notation
\nu_{G}
and satisfying
5
5

5

We abuse notation and denote also by
\langle\,,\rangle
the pairing
X_{*}(\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp})\times X^{*}(\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp})\to\mathds{Z}
.
(see proof of Lemma
2.2.1
)

\langle\nu_{G}(g),\chi|_{\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp}}\rangle=-\omega(\chi(g)),\text{ for all }\chi\in X^{*}(\mathbf{G})_{F}\text{ and }g\in\mathbf{G}(F).

Put
\mathbf{G}(F)^{1}:=\ker\nu_{G}
6
6

6

We refer the reader to §
2.8.2
for more on the homomorphism
\nu_{G}
. Then
\mathbf{G}(F)/\mathbf{G}(F)^{1}
is a finitely generated abelian group and there is an isomorphism

\mathbf{G}(F)/\mathbf{G}(F)^{1}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}\simeq X_{*}(\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q}.

Set
V_{G}:=X_{*}(\mathbf{Z}_{c,sp})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{R}
and let
\mathds{A}_{G}
be a fixed affine space under
V_{G}
.
We have a morphism
\nu_{G}\colon\mathbf{G}(F)\to\textit{\emph{\text{Aff}}}(\mathds{A}_{G})
sending any
g
to the translation
(a\mapsto\nu_{G}(g)+a)
.

We define the extended standard apartment
\mathds{A}_{ext}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
to be the product of
\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})\times\mathds{A}_{G}
together with the group homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{N,ext}\colon\mathbf{N}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textit{\emph{\text{Aff}}}(\mathds{A}_{ext}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{N}(n)\oplus\nu_{G}(n).}

The decomposition
X_{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{R}=V\oplus V_{G}
shows that the extended standard apartment
\mathds{A}_{ext}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
(as defined above) is actually an affine space under
X_{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{R}
and the restriction of
\nu_{N,ext}
to
\mathbf{M}(F)
corresponds precisely to the translation action given by the homomorphism
\nu_{M}
of Lemma
2.2.1
.
In particular,

\mathbf{M}(F)^{1}=\ker\nu_{M}=\ker\nu\cap\mathbf{G}(F)^{1}=\ker\nu_{N}\cap\mathbf{G}(F)^{1}=\ker\nu_{N,ext}.

Remark 2.7.1
.

While the reduced system
(\mathds{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S}),\nu_{N})
is canonical, that is, unique up to unique isomorphism (Proposition
2.2.2
), the extended system
(\mathds{A}_{ext}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S}),\nu_{N,ext})
is only unique up to isomorphism. We ”canonify” it (following G. Rousseau) by viewing (which we will adopt from now on)
\mathds{A}_{G}
as
V_{G}
with a marked origin
\{0_{V_{G}}\}
.

Since there is a one-to-one correspondence between systems of positive roots for
\Phi(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
and vectorial chambers in
\mathds{A}_{ext}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
with apex the fixed special point
a_{\circ}
, we may and will refine our choice of the alcove
\mathfrak{a}
to be the unique alcove with apex
a_{\circ}
such that

\{\alpha\in\Phi\colon\alpha\text{ is $\mathfrak{a}$-positive}\}=\Phi^{+}.

2.7.2.
The extended building

Definition 2.7.2
.

The extended building is the following product of a poly-simplicial complex and a real vector space

\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{ext}=\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}\times V_{G}.

The group
\mathbf{G}(F)
acts isometrically on it as follows:

g\cdot(x,v)=(g\cdot x,v+\nu_{G}(g))\quad\forall g\in\mathbf{G}(F),x\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red},v\in V_{G}.

A facet (resp. alcove, Weyl chamber, apartment) of
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{ext}
is defined to be a product of a same object in
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
and
V_{G}
.
For instance let us fix the extended apartment
\mathcal{A}_{ext}=\mathcal{A}\times V_{G}
.
We identify
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
with the subset
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}\times V_{G}
in
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{ext}
. Then, the stabilizer of
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
for the action of
\mathbf{G}(F)
in the extended building is exactly
\ker\nu_{G}
.
In this building the minimal dimensional facets are of the form
\mathcal{F}_{x}:=\{x\}\times V_{G}
for some vertex
x\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
.
The stabilizer of
(x,0)
is equal to
P_{x}\cap\ker\nu_{G}
, which is also the fixator of
\mathcal{F}_{x}
, where
P_{x}
is the subgroup defined in §
2.3
.

2.8.
Parahoric subgroups

2.8.1.
Further notations

Let
F^{un}
denote the maximal unramified extension of
F
contained in
F^{sep}
and
L
the completion of
F^{un}
with respect to the valuation on
F^{un}
which extends the normalized valuation on
F
. The residue field of
F
is perfect, thus
F^{un}=F^{\text{sh}}
is the strict henselization of
F
in the fixed separable closure
F^{sep}
.
Let
L^{sep}
the separable closure of
L
containing
F^{sep}
.
The arithmetic Frobenius automorphism
\sigma\in\text{Gal}(F^{un}/F)
(which induces
x\mapsto x^{q}
on the residue field of
F^{un}
) extends continuously to an automorphism of
L
over
F
, also denoted
\sigma
.
Write
\text{In}=\text{Gal}(F^{sep}/F^{un})
for the inertia subgroup of
\text{Gal}(F^{sep}/F)
. Since
{L}^{sep}=L\otimes_{F^{un}}{F}^{sep}
, one can identify the inertia subgroup
In
with
\text{Gal}(L^{sep}/L)
.

2.8.2.
The Kottwitz homomorphism

Let
\mathbf{H}
be any connected reductive
F
-group,
\widehat{\mathbf{H}}
be its connected Langlands dual and
Z(\widehat{\mathbf{H}})
be the center of
\widehat{\mathbf{H}}
.
Kottwitz defines in
[
Kot97
, §7.7]
a functorial surjective homomorphism

\kappa_{\mathbf{H}}:\mathbf{H}({L})\rightarrow X^{*}(Z(\widehat{\mathbf{H}})^{\text{In}})=X^{*}(Z(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}))_{\text{In}},

where, the subscript
In
indicates coinvariants of the
\text{Gal}(F^{sep}/F)
-module
X^{*}(Z(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}))
, this latter is the Borovoi fundamental algebraic group (usually denoted
\pi_{1}(\mathbf{H})
) and it is isomorphic to the quotient
X_{*}(\mathbf{T})
for a maximal
F
-torus
\mathbf{T}
by the coroots lattice. The group
\pi_{1}(\mathbf{H})
acquires an action of
\text{Gal}(F^{sep}/F)
via its representation as
X_{*}(\mathbf{T})/\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi(\mathbf{H}_{F^{sep}},\mathbf{T})}\mathds{Z}\alpha^{\vee}
. There is a canonical surjective homomorphism

q_{\mathbf{H}}\colon X^{*}(Z(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}))_{\text{In}}\to\text{Hom}_{\mathds{Z}}(X^{*}(\mathbf{H})^{\text{In}},\mathds{Z})

whose kernel is the torsion subgroup of
X^{*}(Z(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}))_{\text{In}}

[
Kot97
, 7.4.4]
7
7

7

The codomain of (7.4.4) in
loc. cit.
is actually
\text{Hom}_{\mathds{Z}}(X_{*}(Z(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}))^{\text{In}},\mathds{Z}))
, so one needs to use the isomorphism
X_{*}(Z(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}))\simeq X^{*}(\mathbf{H})
.
.
Kottwitz shows in
[
Kot97
, §7.4]
that the above two homomorphisms sit in the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{H}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{*}(Z(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}))_{\text{In}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Hom}_{\mathds{Z}}(X^{*}(\mathbf{H})^{\text{In}},\mathds{Z})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{\mathbf{H}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\nu_{\mathbf{H}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\mathbf{H}}}

where,
\nu_{\mathbf{H}}
is the natural homomorphism characterized by

\langle\nu_{\mathbf{H}}(h),\chi\rangle=\nu_{\mathbf{H}}(h)(\chi)=-\omega(\chi(h)),\text{ for all }h\in\mathbf{H}(L)\text{ and }\chi\in X^{*}(\mathbf{H})^{\text{In}}.

Therefore,
\mathbf{H}(L)_{1}:=\ker\kappa_{\mathbf{H}}\subset\mathbf{H}(L)^{1}:=\ker\nu_{\mathbf{H}}
and

\left(X^{*}(Z(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}))_{\text{In}}\right)_{\text{tors}}=\mathbf{H}(L)^{1}/\mathbf{H}(L)_{1}.

Remark 2.8.1
.

Note here that
\nu_{\mathbf{H}}
differs in a sign from the map
v_{H}
in
[
Kot97
, 7.4.3]
.

Since our connected reductive group
\mathbf{H}
is defined over
F
, the restriction of
\kappa_{\mathbf{H}}
to
\mathbf{H}(F)
provides a surjective homomorphism
\kappa_{H}
that sits in the following commutative diagram (see
[
Kot97
, §7.7]
for the surjectivity of
\kappa_{H}
, see also
[
Ros15
, §2.7]
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{H}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X^{*}(Z(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}))_{\text{In}})^{\langle\sigma\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Hom}_{\mathds{Z}}(X^{*}(\mathbf{H})_{F},\mathds{Z})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\nu_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{H}}

We denote by
\mathbf{H}(F)_{1}=\mathbf{H}(F)\cap\mathbf{H}(L)_{1}
and
\mathbf{H}(F)^{1}
the kernel of
\kappa_{H}
and
\nu_{H}
, respectively.
Therefore, by
[
HV15
, §3.2, Lemma]

\mathbf{H}(F)^{1}=\mathbf{H}(L)^{1}\cap\mathbf{H}(F)=\left\{h\in\mathbf{H}(F)\colon\kappa_{H}(h)\in\left(X^{*}(Z(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}))_{\text{In}}^{\sigma}\right)_{\text{tors}}\right\}.

Set
\Lambda_{H}:=\mathbf{H}(F)/\mathbf{H}(F)_{1}
, thus

\mathbf{H}(F)^{1}/\mathbf{H}(F)_{1}=(\Lambda_{H})_{\text{tors}}\simeq\left(X^{*}(Z(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}))_{\text{In}}^{\sigma}\right)_{\text{tors}}.

2.8.3.
Passage to completion and descent

Consider the extended building
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F^{un})_{ext}
of the group
\mathbf{G}_{F^{un}}=\mathbf{G}\times_{F}F^{un}
. This building is equipped with an action of
\mathbf{G}(F^{un})\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle
. Moreover, there is a natural
\mathbf{G}(F)
-equivariant embedding
\iota\colon\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{ext}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F^{un})_{ext}
such that
[
BT84
, 5.1.25]

\iota(\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{ext})=\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F^{un})_{ext}^{\sigma}.

In other words, the extended building of
\mathbf{G}
over
F
is identified with the fixed points of
\sigma\in\text{Gal}(F^{un}/F)
(in particular of
\sigma
) in the building of
\mathbf{G}
over
F^{un}
.

Since
F^{un}
is henselian, Rousseau has shown in
[
Rou77
, proposition 2.3.9]
that
\mathbf{G}
has the same relative rank over
F^{un}
and over
L
and hence by
[
Rou77
, proposition 2.3.9]
the building
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F^{un})_{ext}
identifies canonically with
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},L)_{ext}
as
\mathbf{G}(F^{un})
-space. Using this identification, we see that the extended building of
\mathbf{G}
over
F
, can also be identified with the fixed points of
\sigma\in\text{Aut}(L/F)
in the building of
\mathbf{G}
over
L
, i.e.

\iota(\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{ext})=\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},L)_{ext}^{\sigma}.

This gives a bijection between the set of
\sigma
-stable facets in
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},L)_{ext}
and the set of facets in
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{ext}
.
For more details one can consult
[
Pra17
]
.

2.8.4.
F
-Parahoric subgroups

In
[
BT84
, §4.6.28, §5.2.6, §5.1.29 and §5.1.30]
(see also
[
Yu15
, §9.3]
), Bruhat and Tits associated two subgroups to any non-empty
\sigma
-stable set
\Upomega
that is contained in an apartment of
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F^{un})_{ext}
and with bounded image in the reduced building:

(1)

The fixator subgroup

\text{Fix}(\mathbf{G}(F^{un}),\Upomega):=\{g\in\mathbf{G}(F^{un})\colon g\cdot a=a,\forall a\in\Upomega\}\subset\mathbf{G}(F^{un}).

They also showed in
loc. cit.
, the existence of a unique affine smooth group scheme
\mathbb{P}_{\Upomega}

defined over
\text{Spec}\,{\mathcal{O}_{F}}
with generic fiber
\mathbf{G}
and which is uniquely characterized by the property
\mathbb{P}_{\Upomega}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{un}})=\text{Fix}(\mathbf{G}(F^{un}),\Upomega)
.

(2)

The ”connected fixator” subgroup

\text{Fix}(\mathbf{G}(F^{un}),\Upomega)^{\circ}:=\mathbb{P}_{\Upomega}^{\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{un}})\subset\text{Fix}(\mathbf{G}(F^{un}),\Upomega),

where
\mathbb{P}_{\Upomega}^{\circ}
is the identity component of
\mathbb{P}_{\Upomega}
.

A
F
-parahoric subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
is by definition
[
BT84
, under 5.2.6]
the ”connected fixator” of a facet
\mathcal{F}\subset\iota(\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{ext})
.

Remark 2.8.2
.

We warn the reader that, in
[
BT84
, §4 and §5]
notations,
\mathbb{P}_{\Upomega}
,
\mathbb{P}_{\Upomega}^{\circ}
,
\text{Fix}(\mathbf{G}(F^{un}),\Upomega)
,
\text{Fix}(\mathbf{G}(F^{un}),\Upomega)^{\circ}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\Upomega}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\Upomega}^{\circ}
are denoted by
\widehat{\mathfrak{G}}_{\Upomega}^{\natural}
,
{\mathfrak{G}}_{\Upomega}^{0\natural}
,
\widehat{P}_{\Upomega}^{1}
and
{P}_{\Upomega}^{0}
, , respectively.

Lemma 2.8.3

Two facets
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{ext}
are equal if and only if

\mathbb{P}_{\iota(\mathcal{F})}^{\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{F})=\text{Fix}(\mathbf{G}(F^{un}),\iota(\mathcal{F}))^{\circ}\cap\mathbf{G}(F)=\text{Fix}(\mathbf{G}(F^{un}),\iota(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}))^{\circ}\cap\mathbf{G}(F)=\mathbb{P}_{\iota(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})}^{\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{F}).

Proof.

See
[
BT84
, 5.2.8]
.
∎

This lemma justifies (by misuse of language) the following definition:

Definition 2.8.4
.

For any bounded subset
\Upomega
contained in an apartment of
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
, set

\widetilde{K}_{\Upomega}=\mathbb{P}_{\iota(\Upomega\times V_{G})}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{un}})\cap\mathbf{G}(F)\text{ and }K_{\Upomega}:=\mathbb{P}_{\iota(\Upomega\times V_{G})}^{\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{F})

A parahoric subgroup of
\mathbf{G}(F)
is
K_{\mathcal{F}}
for some facet
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
.
When the facet
\mathcal{F}
is an alcove
\mathfrak{b}
, the parahoric subgroup
K_{\mathcal{F}}
is called an Iwahori subgroup. The subgroups
\widetilde{K}_{\mathfrak{b}}
and
{K}_{\mathfrak{b}}
will be denoted by
\widetilde{I}_{\mathfrak{b}}
and
{I}_{\mathfrak{b}}
, respectively.

Remark 2.8.5
.

The subgroup
\mathbb{P}_{\Upomega\times V_{G}}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
might be strictly smaller than
\widetilde{K}_{\Upomega}
, see
[
Hai09a
]
for examples.

Haines and Rapoport showed in
[
HR08
]
that parahoric subgroups over
\mathbf{G}(F^{un})
are actually
\mathbf{G}(F^{un})_{1}
-fixators of facets in the Bruhat–Tits building over
F^{un}
.
Accordingly, as we see in the following proposition, this result descend also to the base field
F
, i.e. parahoric subgroups of
\mathbf{G}(F)
are fixators of facets in the kernel of
\kappa_{G}
, i.e. for any facet
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
we have
K_{\mathcal{F}}=P_{\mathcal{F}}\cap\mathbf{G}(F)_{1}
.

Proposition 2.8.6

For any bounded subset
\Upomega
contained in an apartment of
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
we have

\widetilde{K}_{\Upomega}=P_{\Upomega}\cap\mathbf{G}(F)^{1}\text{ and }K_{\Upomega}=\widetilde{K}_{\Upomega}\cap\mathbf{G}(F)_{1}=\mathbb{P}_{\iota(\Upomega\times V_{G})}(\mathcal{O}_{F})\cap\mathbf{G}(F)_{1}

Proof.

On the one hand,

\text{Fix}(\mathbf{G}(F^{un}),\iota(\Upomega\times V_{G}))\cap\mathbf{G}(F)=\text{Fix}(\mathbf{G}(F^{un}),\iota(\Upomega))\cap\mathbf{G}(F^{un})^{1}\cap\mathbf{G}(F).

On the second hand
\text{Fix}(\mathbf{G}(F^{un}),\iota(\Upomega))\cap\mathbf{G}(F)=P_{\Upomega}
(because the map
\iota
is
\mathbf{G}(F)
-invariant and
1
Proposition
2.6.3
), which shows the first equality of the Lemma, since
\mathbf{G}(F^{un})^{1}\cap\mathbf{G}(F)=\mathbf{G}(F)^{1}
.
For the remaining equalities, thanks to
[
HR08
, Proposition 3, remarks 4 and 11]
, one has
\mathbb{P}_{\iota(\Upomega\times V_{G})}^{\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{un}})=\mathbb{P}_{\iota(\Upomega\times V_{G})}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{un}})\cap\mathbf{G}(F^{un})_{1}
, and accordingly:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\iota(\Upomega\times V_{G})}^{\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{F})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\iota(\Upomega\times V_{G})}^{\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{un}})\cap\mathbf{G}(F)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\iota(\Upomega\times V_{G})}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{un}})\cap\mathbf{G}(F^{un})_{1}\cap\mathbf{G}(F)=\mathbb{P}_{\iota(\Upomega\times V_{G})}(\mathcal{O}_{F})\cap\mathbf{G}(F)_{1}.\qed

The map that associates to a facet
\mathcal{F}
its parahoric subgroup
K_{\mathcal{F}}
is decreasing, in particular, if
\mathcal{F}
is any facet of the alcove
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G},F)_{red}
then we have
I_{\mathfrak{a}}\subset K_{\mathcal{F}}
. In addition, the action of an element
g\in\mathbf{G}(F)
on a facet
\mathcal{F}
translates for parahorics to
K_{g\cdot\mathcal{F}}=gK_{\mathcal{F}}g^{-1}
.

2.8.5.
The scheme
\mathcal{Z}

By replacing
\mathbf{G}
by
\mathbf{M}
in all of the above,
we see that
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{M},F)_{red}
is reduced to a single point
\{a_{\circ,M}\}

[
BT84
, §5.1.26]
.
The fixator subgroup
\text{Fix}(\mathbf{M}(F),a_{\circ,M})
is equal to the
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-points of a unique smooth
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-group scheme
\mathcal{Z}
which is the schematic closure of
\mathbf{M}
in
\mathbb{P}_{\iota(\Upomega\times V_{G})}
for any bounded subset
\Upomega\subset\mathbb{A}_{red}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
(see
[
BT84
, 5.2.1 & 5.2.4]
) and the corresponding parahoric subgroup is

K_{a_{\circ,M}}=\text{Fix}(\mathbf{M}(F),a_{\circ,M})^{\circ}=\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{F}).

Applying Proposition
2.8.6
to
\mathbf{M}
and
\Upomega=\{a_{\circ,M}\}
one shows that
K_{a_{\circ,M}}=\mathbf{M}(F)_{1}
.
By
[
BT84
, §5.2.1]
, one has
\mathbf{M}(F)^{1}=\ker\nu\cap\mathbf{G}(F)^{1}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
and in particular
\mathbf{M}(F)_{1}=\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
is of finite index in
\mathbf{M}(F)^{1}
. In summary:

Corollary 2.8.7

The subgroup
\mathbf{M}(F)_{1}
is the unique parahoric subgroup of
\mathbf{M}(F)
and it is a finite index subgroup of
\mathbf{M}(F)^{1}=\ker\nu_{M}
(The maximal open compact subgroup of
\mathbf{M}(F)
).

Lemma 2.8.8

Let
{\mathfrak{b}}\subset\mathcal{A}
be an alcove. The
\mathbf{N}(F)
-fixator of
{\mathfrak{b}}
(resp.
{\mathfrak{b}}\times V_{G}
) is
N_{\mathfrak{b}}=\ker\nu_{N}
(resp.
\mathbf{N}(F)\cap\widetilde{I}_{\mathfrak{b}}=\mathbf{M}(F)^{1}
). Therefore,

P_{\mathfrak{b}}=U_{\mathfrak{b}}^{+}U_{\mathfrak{b}}^{-}\ker\nu\,\text{ and }\widetilde{I}_{\mathfrak{b}}=U_{\mathfrak{b}}^{+}U_{\mathfrak{b}}^{-}\mathbf{M}(F)^{1}.

Proof.

The first equality is stated at the end of
[
BT72
, §4.1.2]
. Here is a geometric argument:
Because
\mathbf{N}(F)
acts transitively on the set of alcoves of
\mathcal{A}
, it suffices to prove the Lemma for
\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{a}
.
Let
n\in\mathbf{N}(F)\cap P_{\mathfrak{a}}
.
By assumption
\nu_{N}(n)(a)=a
for any
a\in\overline{\mathfrak{a}}
, in particular for all
a\in\text{vert}(\mathfrak{a})=\{\text{vertices of $\mathfrak{a}$}\}
.
The set
\left\{a-a_{\circ}\colon a\in\text{vert}(\mathfrak{a})\setminus\{a_{\circ}\}\right\}
forms a basis for
V
and hence
\nu_{N}(n)
must equal the identity on
V
. Therefore,
\mathbf{N}(F)\cap P_{\mathfrak{a}}=\mathbf{M}(F)\cap P_{\mathfrak{a}}=\ker\nu
.
By
3

2.4.5
, this is precisely the
\mathbf{N}(F)
-fixator of
{\mathfrak{a}}
.
Therefore, the first equality in the displayed equation in the Lemma follows from
5
Proposition
2.4.5
and the second one by Proposition
2.8.6
.
∎

2.8.6.
Decompositions

By
[
BT84
, §5.2.4]
, we have the following decomposition of parahoric subgroups:

Proposition 2.8.9

For any bounded subset
\Upomega\subset\mathcal{A}
,
the subgroup
K_{\Upomega}\subset\mathbf{G}(F)
is equal to the subgroup generated by
\mathbf{M}(F)_{1}=\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
and
U_{\Upomega}
, more precisely it has the following decomposition

K_{\Upomega}=U_{\Upomega}\mathbf{M}(F)_{1}=U_{\Upomega}^{+}U_{\Upomega}^{-}U_{\Upomega}^{+}\mathbf{M}(F)_{1}=U_{\Upomega}^{+}U_{\Upomega}^{-}(\mathbf{N}(F)\cap K_{\Upomega}),

such that the factors commute in the right factorization and the product maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi_{red}\cap\Phi^{\pm}}U_{\alpha+f_{{\Upomega}}(\alpha)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\Upomega}^{\pm}=U_{\Upomega}\cap\mathbf{U}(F)^{\pm}=K_{\Upomega}\cap\mathbf{U}(F)^{\pm}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

is an homeomorphism, whatever ordering of the factors we take.

Remark 2.8.10
.

As observed by Haines
[
Hai09a
, §6]
, the reference
[
BT84
, §5.2.4]
contains a typographical error. All of the ”hats” in the four displayed equations in
loc. cit.
should be removed.

Lemma 2.8.11

Let
{\mathfrak{b}}\subset\mathcal{A}
be an alcove. We have
\mathbf{N}(F)\cap I_{\mathfrak{b}}=\mathbf{M}(F)^{1}\cap\mathbf{G}(F)_{1}=\mathbf{M}(F)_{1}
.

Proof.

Clearly,
\mathbf{M}(F)^{1}\subset P_{\mathfrak{b}}
, hence
\mathbf{M}(F)^{1}\cap\mathbf{G}(F)_{1}\subset\mathbf{N}(F)\cap I_{\mathfrak{b}}
. The opposite inclusion follows from Lemma
2.8.8
. This shows the first equality.
For the second, let
m\in\mathbf{M}(F)^{1}\cap\mathbf{G}(F)_{1}=\mathbf{N}(F)\cap I_{\mathfrak{b}}
.
Using the decomposition of Proposition
2.8.9
for
I_{\mathfrak{b}}
, write
m
as
u_{1}^{+}u_{1}^{-}u_{2}^{+}m_{1}\in U_{\mathfrak{b}}^{+}U_{\mathfrak{b}}^{-}U_{\mathfrak{b}}^{+}\mathbf{M}(F)_{1}
, then,
one finds that
mm_{1}^{-1}=\text{ad}(mm_{1}^{-1})(u_{2}^{+})u_{1}^{+}u_{1}^{-}\in\mathbf{M}(F)\cap\mathbf{U}(F)\mathbf{U}^{-}(F)=\{1\}
, i.e.
m=m_{1}\in\mathbf{M}(F)_{1}
.
This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

2.8.7.
The subgroup generated by all parahoric subgroups

As suggested by the condition
[
BT72
, (A 6) §5.2.1]
, Bruhat and Tits introduces in
[
BT84
, §5.2.11]
the subgroup generated by all parahoric subgroups of
\mathbf{G}(F)
denoted here by
\mathbf{G}(F)^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
.
They also prove in
loc. cit.
that
\mathbf{G}(F)^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}=\mathbf{M}(F)_{1}\cdot\varphi(\mathbf{G}_{sc}(F))
, where
\varphi\colon\mathbf{G}_{sc}\to\mathbf{G},
