These are well-known facts for extended affine Weyl groups, so in particular for
\mathcal{H}_{{I}^{1}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])
.
Accordingly, the lemma follows immediately because
\square^{\beta}\colon\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}\to\Lambda_{M}^{1}
is
W
-equivariant and preserves dominance and the length
\ell
. See also
[
Ros15
, Lemma 5.2.1]
.
However, for the reader’s convenience, let us show them using the ingredients we prepared so far:

1. Once we show it for
k=2
(which is obvious), take
m_{\circ}=\sum m_{\circ,i}
such that
m_{\circ,i}+m_{i}\in\Lambda_{M}^{-}
and
m_{\circ,i}\in\Lambda_{M}^{-}
.

2. If
m,m^{\prime}
are both antidominant then by Lemma
3.6.2

I^{\flat}mm^{\prime}I^{\flat}=I^{\flat}mI^{\flat}m^{\prime}I^{\flat}
, hence
\ell(mm^{\prime})=\ell(m)+\ell(m^{\prime})
by Lemma
3.13.2
.

3. If
w\in W
, using this time both Lemma
3.6.2
and Lemma
3.6.1
, we get
I^{\flat}mwI^{\flat}=I^{\flat}mI^{\flat}wI^{\flat}
, hence
\ell(mw)=\ell(m)+\ell(w)
by Lemma
3.13.2
.

4. Thanks to Remark
3.3.4
, this follows from the extended affine case.
∎

3.16.
The Weyl dot-action

Set
\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}:=\mathds{Z}[\Lambda_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}]\subset\mathcal{R}^{\flat}:=\mathds{Z}[\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}]
.

The map
mM^{\flat}\mapsto{\mathbf{1}}_{mM^{\flat}}
yields a natural identification between
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
(resp.
\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}
) and the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(M\sslash M^{\flat},\mathds{Z})
(resp.
\mathcal{H}(M^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}},\flat}\sslash M^{\flat},\mathds{Z})
).
The action of the Weyl group on
\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
extends linearly to an action on
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
.
For integrality reasons that will become clearer in the coming sections, we are interested in a variant of this action:

Definition 3.16.1

(Dot-action)
.

We define a twisted action of
W
on
\mathcal{H}(M\sslash M^{\flat},\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])
, by

\dot{w}(r):=m\mapsto\delta_{B}(m)^{1/2}\delta_{B}(w^{-1}(m))^{-1/2}r(w^{-1}(m)),

for any
w\in W
and any
r\in\mathcal{H}(M\sslash M^{\flat},\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])
.
In particular, for any
m\in M
,

\dot{w}({\bf 1}_{mM^{\flat}})=\delta_{B}(w(m))^{1/2}\delta_{B}(m)^{-1/2}{\bf 1}_{w(m)M^{\flat}}.

Thus, upon identifying
\mathcal{H}(M\sslash M^{\flat},\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])
with
{\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}
, the above dot-action is given on basis elements by

\dot{w}(mM^{\flat})=\left(\frac{\delta_{B}(w(m))}{\delta_{B}(m)}\right)^{1/2}w(m)M^{\flat}\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
,
and extended
\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]
-linearly to
\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
.

Remark 3.16.2
.

(i) From now on, we write
\dot{W}
to specify that we are dealing with the dot-action of
W
and not the standard one.
(ii) The natural projection map
{\square}^{\flat}\colon\Lambda_{M}\to\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
is
\dot{W}
-equivariant, i.e.

(\dot{w}(m))^{\flat}=\dot{w}(m^{\flat})
for any
w\in W
and any
m\in\Lambda_{M}
.

Define
c(m,n):=\delta_{B}(nmn^{-1})^{1/2}\delta_{B}(m)^{-1/2}
for any
m\in M
and any
n\in N
. Note that since the
\delta_{B}
is trivial on the compact
M^{1}
it factors through
\Lambda_{M}^{1}
, and so
c(m,n)
factors then through the image of
(m,n)
in
\Lambda_{M}^{1}\times W
. The following lemma will be used in the sequel.

Lemma 3.16.3

For any
m\in M
and
n\in N
with image
w
in
W
, we have

(i)

c(m,n)=\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi_{red}^{-}\cap w^{-1}(\Phi_{red}^{+})}\delta_{\alpha}(m)\in q^{\mathds{Z}}
.

(ii)

If
m\in M^{-}
then
c(m,n)
is positive power of
q
.

We could have extracted (ii) from a similar result proved in
[
Mac71
, Lemma 3.2.8 & Proposition 3.2.4]
for simply connected groups.

Proof.

(i) Recall that the modulus character
\delta_{B}\colon B=MU^{+}\to q^{\mathds{Z}},mu\mapsto|\det\text{Ad}_{\text{Lie}(U^{+})}(m)|
(it is trivial on
U^{+}
)
13
13

13

Being an absolute value of a determinant on an
F
-vector space, the modulus function has clearly its values in
q^{\mathds{Z}}
.
.
Given that

\text{Lie}(\mathbf{U}^{+})=\oplus_{\alpha\in\Phi_{red}^{+}}\text{Lie}(\mathbf{U}_{\alpha})
, we have

\delta_{B}(m)=\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi_{red}^{+}}\delta_{\alpha}(m),\text{ where }\delta_{\alpha}(m):=|\det\text{Ad}_{\text{Lie}(\mathbf{U}_{\alpha})}(m)|.

Since
n\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}n^{-1}=\mathbf{U}_{w(\alpha)}
, we have
n{\text{Lie}(\mathbf{U}_{\alpha})}n^{-1}={\text{Lie}(\mathbf{U}_{w(\alpha)})}
and so

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}(w(m))=|\det\text{Ad}_{\text{Lie}(\mathbf{U}_{\alpha})}(w(m))|=|\det\text{Ad}_{\text{Lie}(\mathbf{U}_{w^{-1}(\alpha)})}(m)|=\delta_{w^{-1}(\alpha)}(m).

Accordingly,

\displaystyle c(m,n)=\frac{\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi_{red}^{+}}\delta_{w^{-1}(\alpha)}(m)^{1/2}}{\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi_{red}^{+}}\delta_{\alpha}(m)^{1/2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\prod_{\alpha\in w^{-1}(\Phi_{red}^{+})\cap\Phi_{red}^{-}}\delta_{\alpha}(m)^{1/2}}{\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi_{red}^{+}\cap w^{-1}(\Phi_{red}^{-})}\delta_{\alpha}(m)^{1/2}}

\displaystyle={\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi_{red}^{-}\cap w^{-1}(\Phi_{red}^{+})}\delta_{\alpha}(m)}\quad\quad\quad\quad(\delta_{-\alpha}=\delta_{\alpha}^{-1}).

(ii) For any
m\in M^{-}
and any
\alpha\in\Phi_{red}^{-}
, one has
\omega(\delta_{\alpha}(m))\leq 0
. Hence,
c(m,n)\in q^{\mathds{N}}
for any
n\in N
.
∎

4.
Twisted integral Bernstein morphism

4.1.
Freeness of the
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
-module
\mathcal{M}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})

Let
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathbb{A}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})_{red}
be any fixed facet.
Following the ideas of Bernstein
[
BD84
]
, as exposed by the approach of
[
HKP10
]
, we define the universal unramified principal series right
\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
-module
\mathcal{M}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})=\mathcal{C}_{c}(M^{\flat}U^{+}\backslash G/K_{\mathcal{F}},\mathds{Z})
, this is the set of
\mathds{Z}
-valued functions supported on finitely many double cosets.
The
\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
-module structure of
\mathcal{M}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
comes from the natural right action by convolution with respect to the normalized measure
\mu_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}
giving
K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}
volume
1
.

The natural identification between
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\simeq\mathcal{H}(M\sslash M^{\flat},\mathds{Z})

allows us to endow the
\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
-module
\mathcal{M}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
with a left
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
-action as follows: define for any
\psi\in\mathcal{M}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
and
r\in\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
:

r\cdot\psi(g):=\int_{M}r(a)\psi(a^{-1}g)d\mu_{M^{\flat}}(a)\quad(\forall g\in G)

here,
d\mu_{M^{\flat}}(a)
is the Haar measure on
M
giving
M^{\flat}
volume
1
.
We will see in Lemma
4.1.3
a more concrete description of this action, it will confirm that the resulting
r\cdot\psi
is indeed
\mathds{Z}
-valued.
It is clear that the actions of
\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
and
\mathcal{R}
on
\mathcal{M}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
commute:
\mathcal{M}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
is an
(\mathcal{R}^{\flat},\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}))
-bimodule.

Remark 4.1.1
.

Here, we have defined an twisted action without using the modulus character.
The standard action is defined as follows

\displaystyle r\cdot_{\text{twist}}\psi(g)

\displaystyle:=\int_{M}\delta_{B}^{1/2}(a)r(a)\psi(a^{-1}g)d\mu_{M_{1}}(a).\qed

In Theorem
4.2.3
, we will generalize
[
HKP10
, Lemma 1.6.1]
. For this purpose, we begin by the following two lemmas. For any
n\in N
set
v_{n,\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}:={\bf 1}_{U^{+}nK_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}
.

Lemma 4.1.2

The family

\{v_{w,\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}\colon w\in\widetilde{W}^{\flat}/W_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}\},

forms a
\mathds{Z}
-basis for the
\mathds{Z}
-module
\mathcal{M}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of
U^{\pm}\backslash G/K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}\cong\widetilde{W}^{\flat}/W_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}
(Proposition
3.4.1
).
∎

Lemma 4.1.3

For any
w\in N
and
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
we have

m\cdot v_{n,\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}=v_{mn,\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}.

So the action of
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
on
\mathcal{M}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
is induced by the left action of
\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
on
M^{\flat}U^{+}\backslash G/{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}
.

Proof.

Let
n\in N
and
r=mM^{\flat}\in\mathcal{R}
for some
m\in M
. Recall that
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
is identified with the Iwahori–Hecke algebra for
M
;
r=mM^{\flat}\leftrightarrow r={\bf 1}_{mM^{\flat}}
.
The integral defining
r\cdot v_{n,\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}(b)
is (by definition) non-zero only if
b\in mM^{\flat}U^{+}n{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}=M^{\flat}U^{+}mn{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}
, hence
r\cdot v_{n,\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}=\text{s}\,v_{mn,\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}
where
s
is the scalar
r\cdot v_{n,\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}(mw)=|mM^{\flat}|=1
.
∎

The following proposition gives a precise description of the
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
-module
\mathcal{M}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
.

Proposition 4.1.4

The
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
-module
\mathcal{M}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
is free of rank
r_{\mathcal{F}}:=|W/\jmath_{W}(W_{\mathcal{F}})|
, with basis
\{v_{w,\mathcal{F}}^{\flat},\,w\in D_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}\}
where
D_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}\subset W_{a_{\circ}}
is any fixed set of representatives for
W/\jmath_{W}(W_{\mathcal{F}})
. In particular,
\{v_{w,\mathfrak{a}}^{\flat},\,w\in W_{a_{\circ}}\}
is a canonical basis for the
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
-module
\mathcal{M}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
.

Proof.

Given that we have a bijection of
\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
-sets
\widetilde{W}^{\flat}/W_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}\cong\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}\times(W/\jmath_{W}(W_{\mathcal{F}}))
(Lemma
3.3.2
), the proposition follows readily from Lemma
4.1.2
and Lemma
4.1.3
.
∎

Remark 4.1.5
.

Proposition
4.1.4
yields an alternative proof of the main theorem of
[
Lan02
]
giving the dimension of the space of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
-fixed vectors of an unramified principal series representation of
G
.

Remark 4.1.6
.

By Lemma
3.12.1
and Corollary
3.12.2
, the map
\psi^{\flat}\colon\mathcal{M}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathds{Z})\to\mathcal{M}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
,
v\mapsto v*_{K_{\mathcal{F}}}\mathbf{1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}
is an homomorphism of bi-
(\Lambda_{M}-\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}))
-modules.
Here, the left action
\Lambda_{M}
on
\mathcal{M}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})=\mathcal{M}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathds{Z})*_{K_{\mathcal{F}}}\mathbf{1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}
is well defined and factors through
\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
.

4.2.
Properties of the
\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
-module
\mathcal{M}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})

In this subsection we will focus on the case
\mathcal{F}=\mathfrak{a}
, for this write
v_{w,\mathfrak{a}}^{\flat},w\in\widetilde{W}^{\flat}
simply as
v_{w}^{\flat}
.
We would like to describe the structure of
\mathcal{M}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
, this time as a
\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
-module. However, a satisfactory result can only be obtained after enlarging the coefficients ring
\mathds{Z}
to
R:=\mathds{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
.

Proposition 4.2.1

The action of
\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
on
\mathcal{M}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
has the following rules: for any
w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}
and
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
, we have

(1)

v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w}^{\flat}=v_{w}^{\flat}
,

(2)

v_{m}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w}^{\flat}=v_{mw}^{\flat}
,

(3)

v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{m}^{\flat}=v_{m}^{\flat}
if
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat}
.

(4)

v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}(i_{m}^{\flat})^{-1}=v_{-m}^{\flat}\in\mathcal{M}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])
if
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat}
.

Proof.

1 - & 3 - Let
n\in N
. If the quantity
v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{n}^{\flat}(b)=\int_{U^{+}{I}^{\flat}}i_{n}^{\flat}(a^{-1}b)d\mu_{I^{\flat}}(a)
is non-zero for some
b
, then
b\in U^{+}I^{\flat}nI^{\flat}=U^{+}I^{-,\flat}nI^{\flat}
(the equality follows using the Iwahori factorization of
I^{\flat}
and
M^{\flat}\triangleleft N
).

If
n\in N_{1,a_{\circ}}\cup M^{-}
, then we must have
b\in U^{+}nI^{\flat}
by Corollary
3.8.4
.
Accordingly,
v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w}^{\flat}=\text{s}\,v_{w}^{\flat}
, with
s=v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{n}^{\flat}(n)=U^{+}I^{\flat}\cap nI^{\flat}n^{-1}I^{\flat}\in\mathds{Z}
, which is
1
by Corollary
3.8.4
.

2 - Using the previous rule and Lemma
4.1.3
:
v_{m}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w}^{\flat}=m\cdot v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w}^{\flat}=m\cdot v_{w}^{\flat}=v_{mw}^{\flat}
.

4 - Using fact 3, we have
v_{-m}^{\flat}={-m}\cdot v_{1}^{\flat}={-m}\cdot v_{m}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}(i_{m}^{\flat})^{-1}=v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}(i_{m}^{\flat})^{-1}
.
∎

Corollary 4.2.2

For any
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
, any
w\in W
and any
m_{\circ}\in\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat}
such that
m+m_{\circ}\in\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat}
(which always exists by Proposition
3.15.1
), we have in
\mathcal{M}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])

v_{mw}^{\flat}=v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{m+m_{\circ}}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}(i_{m_{\circ}}^{\flat})^{-1}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w}^{\flat}=v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}(i_{m_{\circ}}^{\flat})^{-1}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{m+m_{\circ}}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w}^{\flat}.

Proof.

This follows readily from Proposition
4.2.1
.
∎

Accordingly, any
v\in\mathcal{M}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])
can be written as
v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}h_{v}
for some
h_{v}\in\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])
, i.e.
the following homomorphism of right
\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])
-modules

\displaystyle\hbar_{I^{\flat}}\colon\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}),\quad h\longmapsto v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}h.

is surjective.
Actually, more is true:

Theorem 4.2.3

The homomorphism
\hbar_{I^{\flat}}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Given Corollary
4.2.2
, we just need to show the injectivity of
\hbar_{{I^{\flat}}}
.
In doing so, we will actually show that this map is ”upper triangular with respect to the Chevalley–Bruhat order, with invertible diagonals”.

Recall that
\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
(resp.
\mathcal{M}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
) admits the
\mathds{Z}
-basis
i_{y}^{\flat}
(resp.
v_{x}^{\flat}
) for
x,y\in\widetilde{W}^{\flat}
.
For any
y\in\widetilde{W}^{\flat}
, write
v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{y}^{\flat}=\sum_{x\in\widetilde{W}^{\flat}}c_{x,y}v_{x}^{\flat}
with
c_{x,y}=v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{y}^{\flat}(x)\in\mathds{Z}
.
The function
v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{y}^{\flat}
can be nonzero only on the set
U^{+}I^{\flat}yI^{\flat}
, so by Corollary
3.7.6
one gets

c_{x,y}\neq 0\Leftrightarrow U^{+}x\cap I^{\flat}yI^{\flat}\neq\emptyset\Rightarrow x\leq y.

Therefore,

v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{y}^{\flat}=\sum_{x\in\widetilde{W}^{\flat}\colon x\leq y}c_{x,y}v_{x}^{\flat}.

Now, write any
h\in\ker\hbar_{I^{\flat}}
as
\sum_{y\in\widetilde{W}^{\flat}}r_{y}i_{y}^{\flat}
, where the coefficients
\{r_{y}\}
are nonzero precisely on some finite set
I_{h}\subset\widetilde{W}^{\flat}
.
Assume that
h\neq 0
and let
z\in I_{h}
be any element maximal with respect to the Chevalley–Bruhat order in
I_{h}
.
The coefficient
c_{z,z}=|U^{+}I^{\flat}\cap zI^{\flat}z^{-1}I^{\flat}|_{I^{\flat}}\geq 1
and it is actually a power of
q
by Lemma
3.8.3
.
By maximality of
z
in
I_{h}
, the term
v_{z}^{\flat}
can not be killed in the sum
v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}h=\sum_{y\in I_{h}}\sum_{x\in\widetilde{W}^{\flat}\colon x\leq y}c_{x,y}r_{y}v_{x}^{\flat}
, hence
v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}h
can not equal zero.
Therefore,
\ker\hbar_{I^{\flat}}
is trivial and consequently
\hbar_{I^{\flat}}
is an isomorphism.
∎

Theorem
4.2.3
, implies immediately the following

Corollary 4.2.4

The right
\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
-module
\mathcal{M}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
is free and of rank
1
, with canonical generator
v_{1}^{\flat}
.
This yields a canonical isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{End}_{\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})}(\mathcal{M}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})),\quad h}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}h^{\prime}\mapsto v_{1}*_{{I^{\flat}}}h*_{{I^{\flat}}}h^{\prime}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Corollary 4.2.5

Let
e\in\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])
be an idempotent and set

v_{e}:=v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}e,\mathcal{H}_{e}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}):=e*_{I^{\flat}}\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})*_{I^{\flat}}e\text{ and }\mathcal{M}_{e}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}):=\mathcal{M}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})*_{I^{\flat}}e

The following homomorphism of right
\mathcal{H}_{e}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
-modules is injective

\displaystyle\hbar_{e}\colon\mathcal{H}_{e}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{e}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}),\quad h\longmapsto v_{e}*_{I^{\flat}}h.

Proof.

It is clear that
\hbar_{e}
is a well defined homomorphism of right
\mathcal{H}_{e}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
-modules.
Let
h\in\ker\hbar_{e}
, then
0=v_{e}*_{I^{\flat}}h=v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}h
. Accordingly,
\ker\hbar_{e}\subset\ker\hbar_{I^{\flat}}=\{0\}
by Theorem
4.2.3
.
∎

Remark 4.2.6
.

Let
R
be a ring in which
{|{K^{\flat}}:I^{\flat}|}
is invertible.
We can naturally identify
\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}(R)
with the two-sided ideal
e_{K^{\flat}}*_{{I^{\flat}}}\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}(R)*_{{I^{\flat}}}e_{K^{\flat}}\subset\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}(R)
,
where
e_{K^{\flat}}
is the idempotent
{|{K^{\flat}}:I^{\flat}|}^{-1}{\bf 1}_{K^{\flat}}
(see Lemma
3.11.11
). In a similar way,
\mathcal{M}_{K^{\flat}}(R)
can also be naturally identified with
\mathcal{M}_{{I^{\flat}}}(R)*_{{I^{\flat}}}e_{K^{\flat}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{{I^{\flat}}}(R)
and the right action of
\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}(R)
will correspond to the right action of
e_{K^{\flat}}*_{{I^{\flat}}}\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}(R)*_{{I^{\flat}}}e_{K^{\flat}}
.

4.3.
Decomposition of
\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
and the twisted Bernstein map

We begin this section by considering two sub-algebras of the Iwahori–Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}(\mathds{Z})
:

•

The finite Hecke algebra:

\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}(\mathds{Z})^{0}:=\mathcal{C}_{c}(K^{\flat}\sslash I^{\flat},\mathds{Z}).

A
\mathds{Z}
-basis for
\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}(\mathds{Z})^{0}
is given by
\{i_{w}^{\flat}\}_{w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}}
(Proposition
3.5.1
).

•

The
(\mathcal{R}^{\flat},\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}(\mathds{Z}))
-bimodule structure on
\mathcal{M}_{{I^{\flat}}}(\mathds{Z})
induces a homomorphism of algebras

\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\hookrightarrow\text{End}_{\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}(\mathds{Z})}(\mathcal{M}_{{I^{\flat}}}(\mathds{Z})),

which is actually an embedding since
\mathcal{M}_{{I^{\flat}}}(\mathds{Z})
is free over
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
.
Accordingly, thanks to Theorem
4.2.3
, one gets another embedding of algebras:

\displaystyle\dot{\Theta}_{\text{\text{Bern}}}^{\flat}\colon\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}),\quad m\mapsto\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat},

characterized by the property:
m\cdot v_{1}^{\flat}=v_{1}^{\flat}*_{{I^{\flat}}}\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}
, for any
m\in\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
.

Lemma 4.3.1

For any
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
and any
m_{\circ}\in\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat}
such that
m+m_{\circ}\in\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat}
,
we have

\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}=i_{m+m_{\circ}}^{\flat}*_{{I^{\flat}}}(i_{m_{\circ}}^{\flat})^{-1}=(i_{m_{\circ}}^{\flat})^{-1}*_{{I^{\flat}}}i_{m+m_{\circ}}^{\flat}.

In particular,
\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}=i_{m}^{\flat}
if
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat}
and
\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}=(i_{-m}^{\flat})^{-1}
if
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{+,\flat}
.

Proof.

These equalities follow readily from Corollary
4.2.2
.
∎

Remark 4.3.2
.

(i) By Remark
4.1.6
, since
\mathbf{1}_{I^{\flat}}
is central in
\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])
, the following diagram is commutative

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{I}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\Theta}_{\text{\text{Bern}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\Theta}_{\text{\text{Bern}}}^{\flat}}

In other words,
\iota^{\flat}(\dot{\Theta}_{m})=\dot{\Theta}_{{m}^{\flat}}^{\flat},m\in\mathcal{R}
.

(ii) The Iwahori–Hecke operators
\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}
generalizes the element denoted by
\widetilde{T}
in Lusztig’s
[
Lus83
, §7]
(up to the factor
\delta_{B}(m)^{1/2}
),
which does not appear here. The untwisted version of the map
\dot{\Theta}_{\text{Bern}}
was attributed by Lusztig in
loc. cit.
(for split reductive groups) to Bernstein, this is the reason we use the subscript
Bern
.

Corollary 4.3.3

For any
w\in\widetilde{W}^{\flat}
, we have

i_{w}^{\flat}=\sum_{x=m_{x}w_{x}\leq w}\dot{\Theta}_{m_{x}}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w_{x}}^{\flat}.

Proof.

The lemma follows readily from the proof of Theorem
4.2.3
and Corollary
4.2.2
.
∎

Lemma 4.3.4

For any
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
, on has

(\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat})^{\vee}=(i_{w_{\circ}}^{\flat})^{-1}*_{I^{\flat}}\dot{\Theta}_{-w_{\circ}(m)}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w_{\circ}}^{\flat}

where
w_{\circ}\in W
is the longest element.

Proof.

It suffices to show it for elements in
\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat}
, so let
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat}
we also have
-w_{\circ}(m)\in\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat}
and accordingly by Lemma
3.15.1

\ell(w_{\circ}(-m)\cdot w_{\circ})=\ell(w_{\circ}m^{-1})=\ell(w_{\circ})+\ell(-m)=\ell(w_{\circ})+\ell(w_{\circ}(-m))

hence

\displaystyle i_{-w_{\circ}(m)}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w_{\circ}}^{\flat}

\displaystyle=i_{w_{\circ}(-m)\cdot w_{\circ}}^{\flat}=i_{w_{\circ}m^{-1}}^{\flat}=i_{w_{\circ}}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{-m}^{\flat}.\qed

In our setting, it is easy to obtain a statement similar to
[
HKP10
, Lemma 1.7.1]
:

Lemma 4.3.5

The homomorphism of
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}{\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
-modules

(\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}{\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}^{0}(\mathds{Z})\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}),\quad m\otimes h^{0}\longmapsto\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}*_{{I^{\flat}}}h^{0},

is an isomorphism. Composing this homomorphism with
h\mapsto v_{1}^{\flat}*_{{I^{\flat}}}h
yields the isomorphism of
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}{\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
-modules
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}^{0}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{{I^{\flat}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
,
given by
m\otimes i_{w}^{\flat}\longmapsto v_{mw}^{\flat}
, for
w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}
and
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
.

Proof.

Given Theorem
4.2.3
, this lemma follows immediately from Corollary
4.2.2
which shows that the composition

\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}^{0}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{{I^{\flat}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}),

yields an isomorphism of
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}{\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
-modules.
∎

A direct application of Lemma
4.3.5
gives an explicit
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}{\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
-basis for the Iwahori–Hecke algebra:

Corollary 4.3.6

The algebra
\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
is a free left (and right)
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}{\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
-module, with canonical basis
\{i_{w}^{\flat}\colon w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}\}
.
The sets
\{\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}*_{{I^{\flat}}}i_{w}^{\flat}\colon m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat},w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}\}

and
\{i_{w}^{\flat}*_{{I^{\flat}}}\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}\colon m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat},w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}\}
are both
{\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
-basis for
\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
.

Proof.

The fact that
\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
is a free left
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}{\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
-module, with canonical basis
\{i_{w}^{\flat}\colon w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}\}
and the set
\{\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}*_{{I^{\flat}}}i_{w}^{\flat}\colon m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat},w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}\}
is
{\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
-basis for
\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
is clear from Corollary
4.3.5
. Let us show the remaining statements. We may argue in two different ways:

(i) Consider
\mathcal{M}^{-}_{{I^{\flat}}}(\mathds{Z})=\mathds{Z}[I^{\flat}\backslash G/U^{-}]
instead, this is a
(\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}),\mathcal{R}^{\flat})
-bi-module.
Rewriting the whole story of §
4
in this context shows that
\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
is a free right
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}{\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
-module and so
\{i_{w}^{\flat}*_{{I^{\flat}}}\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}\colon m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat},w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}\}
is
{\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
-basis.

(ii) The map
\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}^{0}(\mathds{Z})\to\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}^{0}(\mathds{Z}),i_{w}^{\flat}\to i_{w_{\circ}}^{\flat}*_{{I^{\flat}}}(i_{w}^{\flat})^{\vee}
is an isomorphism of
\mathds{Z}
-modules, the map
\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}\to\Lambda_{M}^{\flat},m\mapsto-w_{\circ}(m)
is an automorphism of rings and
\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])\to\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]),h\mapsto((i_{w_{\circ}}^{\flat})^{-1}*_{I^{\flat}}h)^{\vee}
is an automorphism of
\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]
-modules.
Therefore, the homomorphism of
{\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
-modules

(\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}{\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}^{0}(\mathds{Z})\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}),\quad m\otimes i_{w}^{\flat}\longmapsto\left((i_{w_{\circ}}^{\flat})^{-1}*_{{I^{\flat}}}\dot{\Theta}_{-w_{\circ}(m)}^{\flat}*_{{I^{\flat}}}i_{w_{\circ}}^{\flat}*_{{I^{\flat}}}(i_{w}^{\flat})^{\vee}\right)^{\vee},

is an isomorphism. Given Lemma
4.3.4
, this concludes the proof of the corollary.
∎

Remark 4.3.7
.

If we replace
G
by
G_{1}^{\flat}
then
\Lambda_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}
plays exactly the role of
\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
. For example, as in Corollary
4.3.5
, we have two decomposition:

\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])=\bigoplus_{w\in W}i_{w}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}\Theta_{\text{Bern}}^{\flat}(\Lambda_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])=\bigoplus_{w\in W}\Theta_{\text{Bern}}^{\flat}(\Lambda_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w}^{\flat}.

4.4.
Further properties of
\dot{\Theta}^{\flat}
-elements

By Corollary
3.14.1
, various results in
[
Lus89
]
applies to
\mathcal{H}_{I^{1}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
(i.e.
M^{\flat}=M^{1}
) and
\mathcal{H}_{I}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
. In this subsection, we will extend some results proved by Lusztig for
\mathcal{H}_{I^{1}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])
to
\mathcal{H}_{{I}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])
.

From now on, whenever needed, we identify
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
with its image
\dot{\Theta}_{\text{\text{Bern}}}^{\flat}(\mathcal{R}^{\flat})
and
\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}
with
\dot{\Theta}_{\text{\text{Bern}}}^{\flat}(\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat})
.
Let
\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}
be the fraction field of
\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}
.

Lemma 4.4.1

We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}\mathcal{H}_{I}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{1}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{1}}\mathcal{H}_{I^{1}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{1}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{1}}\mathcal{H}_{I^{1}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\flat}}

Proof.

By Remark
3.13.5
, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{{I}^{1}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{{I}^{1}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{1}}

Therefore, It remains to show why the horizontal ones and the upper vertical ones are injective.
For this, we only need to show that no non-zero element in
\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}
is a zero left/right divizor in
\mathcal{H}_{I}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
:
Let
a\in\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}
and
b\in\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
such that
a*_{I^{\flat}}b=0
.
By the decomposition of Remark
3.13.4
,
b=\sum_{\sigma\in\widetilde{\Upomega}^{\flat}}\sigma\otimes b_{\sigma}
for some
b_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
. Hence
\sigma(a)*_{I^{\flat}}b_{\sigma}=0
for all
\sigma\in\widetilde{\Upomega}^{\flat}
.
Thanks to the decomposition
\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})=\oplus_{w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}}(\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w}^{\flat}
(Remark
4.3.7
) we see that
a
must be a zero-divisor in
\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}
which forces it to be zero since this latter is a domain.
If we start with a right zero, i.e.
b*_{I^{\flat}}a=0
, then using decomposition
\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})=\oplus_{w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}}i_{w}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}(\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])
one shows similarly that
a
must be zero.
∎

Lemma 4.4.2

For any
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat},w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}
and
s\in\mathcal{T}_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}=\mathcal{S}^{\flat}\cap W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}
, we have
\dot{\Theta}_{-m}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}\dot{\Theta}_{s(m)}^{\flat}\in\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
.

Proof.

For any
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
and
s\in\mathcal{T}_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}
, by
1
Proposition
3.15.1
, one can find an element
m_{\circ}\in\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat}
such that
m_{\circ}-m
and
m_{\circ}+s(m)
both lie in
\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat}
. Hence, for any
w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}
, we have

\dot{\Theta}_{-m}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}\dot{\Theta}_{s(m)}^{\flat}=(i_{m_{\circ}}^{\flat})^{-1}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{(m_{\circ}-m)w}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{m_{\circ}+s(m)}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}(i_{m_{\circ}}^{\flat})^{-1}.

Let
z_{m_{\circ}}
be the projection of
m_{\circ}
in
\widetilde{\Upomega}^{\flat}
.
By
5
Corollary
3.13.3
, we have

i_{m_{\circ}}^{\flat}\in\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})^{\times}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{z_{m_{\circ}}}^{\flat}=i_{z_{m_{\circ}}}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})^{\times}.

Accordingly, the support

\text{Supp}(\dot{\Theta}_{-m}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}\dot{\Theta}_{s(m)}^{\flat})\subset I^{\flat}z_{m_{\circ}}^{-1}m_{\circ}m^{-1}wI^{\flat}m_{\circ}s(m)z_{m_{\circ}}^{-1}I^{\flat}\subset G_{1}^{\flat}.

Therefore,
\dot{\Theta}_{-m}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}\dot{\Theta}_{s(m)}\in\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
.
∎

Proposition 4.4.3

For any
s\in\mathcal{T}_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}
and any
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
, we have

\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}(i_{s}^{\flat}+1)-(i_{s}^{\flat}+1)*_{I^{\flat}}\dot{\Theta}_{\dot{s}(m)}^{\flat}=(\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}-\dot{\Theta}_{\dot{s}(m)}^{\flat})\mathcal{G}(\alpha)\in\mathcal{R}^{\flat}

where,
\mathcal{G}(\alpha)\in\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}
is the element defined in
[
Lus89
, §3.8 & §3.13]
for the algebra
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{D}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}},{W}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}},v=q,L)
.

Proof.

Let
m\in M
. We want to apply
[
Lus89
, Proposition 3.9]
. Since in
loc. cit.
the notation
{\theta}_{m}
does not equal our
\dot{\Theta}_{m}
, we first compare the two to make things clear. Let
m_{1},m_{2}\in M^{-}
verifying
m=m_{1}-m_{2}
, hence by Corollary
3.14.1
and
[
Lus89
, Definition §3.3 (a)]

{\theta}_{m}=\frac{q_{m_{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{q_{m_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}}i_{m_{1}}*_{I}i_{m_{2}}^{-1}\overset{\text{Lemma \ref{qmdeltam}}}{=}\delta_{B}(m)^{\frac{1}{2}}\dot{\Theta}_{m}.

Set
A_{s}(m):=(i_{s}+1)-\theta_{-m}(i_{s}+1)\theta_{s(m)}-(1-\theta_{s(m)-m})\mathcal{G}(\alpha)
.
Clearly, the term
(1-\theta_{s(m)-m})\mathcal{G}(\alpha)\in\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
, but its image by
\iota^{1}
lies in
\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{1}

[
Lus89
, §2.5 (b)]
, hence
(1-\theta_{s(m)-m})\mathcal{G}(\alpha)\in\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
.
Accordingly, by Lemma
4.4.2
, we have
A_{s}(m)\in\mathcal{H}_{I}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
.

Thanks to
[
Lus89
, Proposition 3.9]
, we know that
\iota^{1}(A_{s}(m))=0
in
\mathcal{H}_{I^{1}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
since by Remark
4.3.2
we have
\iota^{1}(\dot{\Theta}_{m})=\dot{\Theta}_{{m}^{1}}^{1}
(recall that
m^{1}
denotes the image of
m
in
\Lambda_{M}^{1}
) .
Therefore
A_{s}(m)=0
, given that
\iota^{1}\colon\mathcal{H}_{I}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})\to\mathcal{H}_{I^{1}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
is injective.

In conclusion, we proved that for any reflexion
s\in\mathcal{T}_{a_{\circ}}
and any
m\in M

\dot{\Theta}_{m}(i_{s}+1)-c(m,s)(i_{s}+1)\dot{\Theta}_{s(m)}=(\dot{\Theta}_{m}-c(m,s)\dot{\Theta}_{s(m)})\mathcal{G}(\alpha)

which is proves the lemma (see Remark
4.3.2
).
∎

4.5.
\dot{W}
-invariant elements are central

For
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat}
, set

r_{m}^{\flat}:=\sum_{w\in W/W_{m}}\dot{w}(m)=\sum_{w\in W/W_{m}}c(m,w)w(m)

where,
W_{m}
denotes the isotropy subgroup of
m
in
W
(for the standard Weyl action).

Let
{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\subset{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}
denotes the
\mathds{Z}
-subalgebra of elements invariant under the dot-action, which again will be identified with its
\dot{\Theta}_{\text{\text{Bern}}}^{\flat}({\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}})
.
So
r_{m}^{\flat}
also denotes its image
\sum_{w\in W/W_{m}}\dot{\Theta}_{\dot{w}(m)}^{\flat}=\sum_{w\in W/W_{m}}c(m,w)\dot{\Theta}_{{w}(m)}^{\flat}
.

By Lemma
3.16.3
, all
r_{m}\in{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}
for all
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat}
.
In consequence, Lemma
3.4.3
implies that
\{r_{m}^{\flat},m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat}\}
is a
\mathds{Z}
-basis for
{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}
.

Corollary 4.5.1

For any
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
and any
s\in\mathcal{T}_{a_{\circ}}
,
\dot{\Theta}_{m}^{\flat}+\dot{\Theta}_{\dot{s}(m)}^{\flat}
commutes with
i_{s}^{\flat}
.
In consequence,

{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\subset Z(\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})).

Proof.

The first statement is an immediate consequence of Proposition
4.4.3
.
For the second statement,
we observe that for any
s\in\mathcal{T}_{a_{\circ}}
(Definition
2.5.2
) and any
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
, we have

|W/W^{m}|r_{m}^{\flat}=\sum_{w\in\dot{W}}\dot{\Theta}_{\dot{w}(m)}^{\flat}=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{P}_{s}^{l}\cap{W}}\dot{\Theta}_{\dot{w}(m)}^{\flat}+\dot{\Theta}_{\dot{s}\dot{w}(m)}^{\flat}.

So
r_{m}^{\flat}
commutes with
i_{s}^{\flat}
for all
s\in\mathcal{S}^{\flat}
.
This completes the proof since
W
is generated by
\mathcal{S}^{\flat}\cap W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}
.
∎

5.
Twisted integral Satake morphisms

5.1.
Twisted Satake homomorphism: first basic properties

In this section, we focus on the level
K^{\flat}
.
By Proposition
4.1.4
,
the
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
-module
\mathcal{M}_{K^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
is free of rank
1
(with canonical basis element the spherical vector
v_{1,K}^{\flat}:=v_{1,a_{\circ}}^{\flat}={\bf 1}_{U^{+}K^{\flat}}
).
Accordingly, we have a
\mathds{Z}
-algebra homomorphism
\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})\to\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
, which we denote by
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}
. It is called the Satake transform and is characterized by

v_{1,K}^{\flat}*_{K^{\flat}}h=\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}(h)\cdot v_{1,K}^{\flat},\text{ for all }h\in\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}).

This is actually an embedding of
\mathds{Z}
-algebras (using the fact that
v_{1,K}^{\flat}=v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}{\bf 1}_{K^{\flat}}
and Corollary
4.2.5
). In particular, the algebra
\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
is a commutative.

Proposition 5.1.1

The image of
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}
is
\dot{W}
-invariant, i.e.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}\colon\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}.}

Proof.

We give two proofs in the following two subsubsections; the first is ”algebraic” while the second is of ”analytical” taste. The first is short while the second is explicit:

First, note that by Corollary
3.12.2
and Remark
3.16.2
, it is sufficient to show
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}\colon\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}}^{\dot{W}}.}

5.1.1.
Algebraic argument

Proof.

Let
h\in\mathcal{H}_{K}(\mathds{Z})\subset\mathcal{H}_{I}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])
.
We have

v_{1}*_{I}h=v_{1,K}*_{K}h=\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)\cdot v_{1,K}=\sum_{w\in W_{a_{\circ}}}\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)\cdot v_{w}.

Hence,
h=\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)*_{I}\sum_{w\in W_{a_{\circ}}}i_{w}=\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)*_{I}{\bf 1}_{K}
.
Now, because
h
is
K
-bi-equivariant, we have for any
s\in\mathcal{T}_{a_{\circ}}

i_{s}*_{I}h=i_{s}*_{I}{\bf 1}_{K}*_{K}h=h=h*_{I}i_{s}.

Therefore, for any
s\in\mathcal{S}\cap W_{a_{\circ}}
:

0=i_{s}*_{I}\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)*_{I}{\bf 1}_{K}-\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)*_{I}i_{s}*_{I}{\bf 1}_{K}=\left(i_{s}*_{I}\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)-\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)*_{I}i_{s}\right)*_{I}\sum_{w\in W_{a_{\circ}}}i_{w}.

Therefore, given that
i_{s}*_{I}\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)-\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)*_{I}i_{s}\in\mathcal{R}
by Proposition
4.4.3
,
by Corollary
4.3.6
we must have

i_{s}*_{I}\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)-\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)*_{I}i_{s}=0,\forall s\in\mathcal{T}_{a_{\circ}}.

This concludes the first proof of the proposition, since
W_{a_{\circ}}
is generated by
\mathcal{T}_{a_{\circ}}
.
∎

5.1.2.
Analytical argument

First, we need an explicit integral formula for the Satake transform:

Lemma 5.1.2

Let
h
be any function in
\mathcal{H}_{K}(\mathds{Z})
, then its Satake twisted transform is explicitly given by

\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)\colon m\mapsto\int_{U^{+}}h(um)d\mu_{U^{+}}(u),

where
d\mu_{U^{+}}
is the Haar measure on
U^{+}
giving
1
on
U^{+}\cap K
.

Proof.

We just need to evaluate
v_{1,K}*_{K}h=\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)\cdot v_{1,K}
on both sides:
On the one hand, we have for
m\in M

\displaystyle(\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)\cdot v_{1,K})(m)=\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)(m).

On the second hand we have
14
14

14

We refer to
[
Car79
, §4.1]
for all integration formulas used in the sequel
thanks to the Iwasawa decomposition
G=BK
(Corollary
3.4.2
)

\displaystyle(v_{1,K}*_{K}h)(m)

\displaystyle=\int_{B}\int_{K}v_{1,K}(bk)h(k^{-1}b^{-1}m)d\mu_{B}(b)\,dk

\displaystyle=\int_{B}v_{1,K}(b)h(b^{-1}m)d\mu_{B}(b)\quad\quad\quad(K\text{ invariance})

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\int_{U^{+}}v_{1,K}(au)h(u^{-1}a^{-1}m)d\mu_{M}(a)d\mu_{U^{+}}(u)

\displaystyle=\int_{U^{+}}h(u^{-1}m)d\mu_{U^{+}}(u)\quad\quad\quad\quad(v_{1,K}(au)\neq 0\Rightarrow a\in M_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{U^{+}}h(um)d\mu_{U^{+}}(u)\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad(U^{+}\text{ is unimodular})

Where,
d\mu_{B}
(respectively
d\mu_{U^{+}}
and
dk
) is the Haar left invariant measure on
B
(respectively
U^{+}
and
K
) giving volume
1
to
B\cap K
(respectively
U^{+}\cap K
and
K
).
∎

Remark 5.1.3
.

The function
m\mapsto\det(\text{Ad}_{\text{Lie}(U^{+})}(m)-\text{Id}_{\text{Lie}(U^{+})})
is polynomial and nonzero.
The set of regular elements is, by definition, the dense set of elements of
M
which do not annihilate the nonzero polynomial function
m\mapsto\det(\text{Ad}_{\text{Lie}(U^{+})}(m)-\text{Id}_{\text{Lie}(U^{+})})
.

Lemma 5.1.4

Let
h
be any function in
\mathcal{H}_{K}(\mathds{Z})
and
m\in M
any regular elements (Definition
5.1.3
). Then

\int_{U^{+}}h(um)d\mu_{U^{+}}(u)=\Delta(m)\int_{G/S}h(gmg^{-1})\frac{d\mu_{K}(g)}{ds}

where, the Haar measure
ds
on
S=\mathbf{S}(F)
is normalized by
\int_{M/S}\frac{d\mu_{M_{1}}}{ds}=1
.

Proof.

The same reasoning of
[
Car79
, Lemma 4.1]
proves the lemma.
∎

Remark 5.1.5
.

As opposed to
[
HR10
, Remark 10.1.1]
, the twisted Satake transform defined here, is dependent on the choice of the minimal parabolic
\mathbf{B}
which contains
\mathbf{M}
as a Levi factor.

Given Lemma
5.1.2
, we need to prove that

\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)(m)=\dot{w}\left(\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)(m)\right)

for all functions
h\in\mathcal{H}_{K}(\mathds{Z})
,
m\in M
and
w\in W
.
By continuity, it suffices to show this equality for all
m
in the dense set of elements of regular (Remark
5.1.3
) which are semi-simple as elements in
G
.
For these elements, we know by Lemma
5.1.4
that

\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)(m)=\Delta(m)\int_{G/S}h(gmg^{-1})\frac{d\mu_{K}(g)}{ds}

On the one hand, by
[
Car79
, (23)]
, one easily shows that
\Delta(\dot{w}(m))=\Delta(m)
.
On the other hand, since
h
is
K
-bi-invariant, one has
h(n_{w}gn_{w}^{-1})=h(g)
for all
g\in G
.
The compact subgroup
N\cap K
acts by inner automorphisms on
G
and on
S
and hence leaves invariant the
G
-measure on
G/S
.
Accordingly,

\dot{w}\left(\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)(m)\right)=\Delta(w\bullet m)\int_{G/S}h(g(n_{w}mn_{w}^{-1})g^{-1})\frac{d\mu_{K}(g)}{ds}=\Delta(m)\int_{G/S}h(gmg^{-1})\frac{d\mu_{K}(g)}{ds}=\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)(m).

This ends the ”analytical” argument for Proposition
5.1.1
.
∎

Remark 5.1.6
.

The following two diagrams are commutative

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{K}(\mathds{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}^{\dot{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{K}(\mathds{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}^{\dot{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\flat}=-*_{K_{\mathcal{F}}}\mathbf{1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}}

Here, the last right vertical map is the natural inclusion
\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}\to\Lambda_{M}
given by
mM^{\flat}\mapsto\sum_{m^{\prime}\in M^{\flat}/M_{1}}mm^{\prime}M_{1}
.

5.2.
Twisted Satake isomorphism

Motivated by arithmetic problems for Shimura varieties, Haines and Rostami already established in
[
HR10
]
,
\mathcal{S}_{M}^{G}\colon\mathcal{H}_{K}(\mathds{C})\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}}\mathcal{R}^{W}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{C}
. In this section, we extend their result to
\mathds{Z}
-coefficients provided by the twisted Satake homomorphism
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}
.

Theorem 5.2.1

The twisted Satake homomorphism
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}\colon\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})\to{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}
is a canonical isomorphism of
{\mathds{Z}}
-algebras.

As for Proposition
5.1.1
, we give in the following two subsubsections two proofs; the first is ”algebraic” while the second is of ”combinatorial” taste.
We will see in §
6
, yet a third method which will give simultaneously this type of isomorphisms for all parahoric subgroup
K_{\mathcal{F}}
with
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathfrak{a}
.

5.2.1.
Algebraic argument

Proof.

Lemma 5.2.2

(Integral compatibility)

The Satake and Bernstein twisted
\mathds{Z}
-homorphisms are compatible, i.e.,
the following diagram (of
\mathds{Z}
-modules) is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}\otimes\text{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\Theta}_{Bern}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-*_{I^{\flat}}{\bf 1}_{K^{\flat}}}

The injectivity of the horizontal and right diagonal maps is Corollary
4.5.1
and Proposition
5.1.1
.
Let
h\in\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}})
, we have

\displaystyle v_{1}*_{I^{\flat}}h

\displaystyle=v_{1}*_{I^{\flat}}e_{K^{\flat}}*_{{I^{\flat}}}h

\displaystyle\quad(e_{K^{\flat}}*_{I^{\flat}}h=h)

\displaystyle={[K^{\flat}:I^{\flat}]}(v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}e_{K^{\flat}})*_{K^{\flat}}h

\displaystyle\quad(\text{passing from $*_{I^{\flat}}$ to $*_{K^{\flat}}$})

\displaystyle={[K^{\flat}:I^{\flat}]}\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}(h)\cdot(v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}e_{K^{\flat}})

\displaystyle(\text{Definition of $\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}$})

\displaystyle={[K^{\flat}:I^{\flat}]}(\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}(h)\cdot v_{1}^{\flat})*_{I^{\flat}}e_{K^{\flat}}

\displaystyle={[K^{\flat}:I^{\flat}]}(v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}\dot{\Theta}_{\text{\emph{Bern}}}^{\flat}({\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}(h)}))*_{I^{\flat}}e_{K^{\flat}}

\displaystyle(\text{Definition of $\dot{\Theta}_{\text{\text{Bern}}}^{\flat}$})

\displaystyle=v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}\dot{\Theta}_{\text{\emph{Bern}}}\circ{\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}(h)}*_{I^{\flat}}\mathbf{1}_{K^{\flat}}

Hence,
h=\dot{\Theta}_{\text{\emph{Bern}}}^{\flat}\circ{\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}(h)}*_{I^{\flat}}\mathbf{1}_{K^{\flat}}=\mathbf{1}_{K^{\flat}}*_{I^{\flat}}\dot{\Theta}_{\text{\emph{Bern}}}^{\flat}\circ{\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}(h)}
.∎

Accordingly, Theorem
5.2.1
, follows readily from:

Lemma 5.2.3

For any
h\in\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
, and any
f\in{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}
we have

h=\dot{\Theta}_{\text{Bern}}^{\flat}(\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}(h))*_{I^{\flat}}{\bf 1}_{K^{\flat}}\text{ and }\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}(\dot{\Theta}_{\text{Bern}}^{\flat}(f)*_{I^{\flat}}{\bf 1}_{K^{\flat}})=f.

In other words,
(-*_{I^{\flat}}{}_{I^{\flat}})\circ\dot{\Theta}_{\text{Bern}}^{\flat}
is the inverse of
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}
.

Let
f\in{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}
so
\dot{\Theta}_{\text{Bern}}^{\flat}(f)\in Z(\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]))
.
Then, clearly
h_{f}=\dot{\Theta}_{\text{Bern}}^{\flat}(f)*_{I^{\flat}}{\bf 1}_{K^{\flat}}={\bf 1}_{K^{\flat}}*_{I^{\flat}}\dot{\Theta}_{\text{Bern}}^{\flat}(f)\in\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
.
Now, given that
v_{1,K}^{\flat}*_{K^{\flat}}h_{f}=v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}h_{f}=f\cdot v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}{\bf 1}_{K^{\flat}}
, one concludes that
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}(h_{f})=f.

If we start with any element
h\in\mathcal{H}_{K^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z})
, the same reasoning shows that

\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}(h)=\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}(\dot{\Theta}_{\text{Bern}}^{\flat}\circ\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}(h)*_{I^{\flat}}{\bf 1}_{K^{\flat}})

and Proposition
5.1.1
then implies
h=\dot{\Theta}_{\text{Bern}}^{\flat}\circ\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{\flat}}(h)*_{I^{\flat}}{\bf 1}_{K^{\flat}}.

∎

5.2.2.
Combinatorial argument

Thanks to Remark
5.1.6
, it is sufficient to show that
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}\colon\mathcal{H}_{K}(\mathds{Z})\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}}{\mathcal{R}}^{\dot{W}}
(
M^{\flat}=M_{1}
) to prove Theorem
5.2.1
. So, we focus on this case in this subsection.

Definition 5.2.4
.

Let
\precsim^{\flat}
be the partial order on
\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
defined as follows

m\succsim^{\flat}m^{\prime}\text{ if and only if }m-m^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\text{ and }\nu(m-m^{\prime})=\sum_{\Delta^{\vee}}n_{\alpha}\alpha^{\vee},\text{ with }n_{\alpha}\in\mathds{N}.

Remark 5.2.5
.

(i) When
M^{\flat}=M^{1}
, this is the usual order on the lattice
\Lambda_{M}^{1}
.
(ii) Note that if
m,m^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{M}
then
m^{\prime}\precsim m
if and only if
m^{\prime\flat}\precsim^{\flat}m^{\flat}
, because (i)
\nu
factors through
M/\ker\nu
and a fortiori through
\Lambda_{M}^{1}
, and (ii) because
\square^{\flat}\colon\Lambda_{M}\twoheadrightarrow\Lambda_{M}^{\flat}
restricts to an isomorphism
\Lambda_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}\to\Lambda_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}
.

!

Lemma 5.2.6

If
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-}
, then
U^{+}m^{-1}K\cap Km^{-1}K=m^{-1}K
.

Proof.

We intend to use
[
BT72
, 4.3.6 Corollaire]
for
G_{1}
by taking (with
loc. cit.
notations)
\Upomega=\{a_{\circ}\}
and
\Upomega^{\prime}=\{a_{\circ}+\nu(m)\}
and
n=1
. Since
\Upomega\subset\Lambda_{M}^{-}
, we have
a_{\circ}\subset\Upomega^{\prime}+\overline{\mathcal{C}}^{+}
, so the condition of
[
BT72
, 4.3.6 Corollaire]
is fulfilled and we have
U^{+}K\cap K_{a_{\circ}+\nu(m)}K=K
. This shows the lemma.
∎

Lemma 5.2.7

Let
m,m^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{M}
, if
U^{+}m^{\prime}\cap KmK\neq\emptyset
then
m^{\prime}\precsim m
.

Proof.

If the above intersection is nonempty, i.e.
u\in U^{+}\cap{m^{\prime}}^{-1}KmK
.
There exists then
m_{0}\in M
such that
m_{0}um_{0}^{-1}\in I
, hence, the alcove
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}=\nu(m_{0})(\mathfrak{a})
verifies

I_{\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}}m^{\prime}m^{-1}\cap KK_{a_{\circ}+\nu(m)}\neq\emptyset

So in particular,
m-m^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
. We may then apply
[
BT72
, 4.3.16 Corollaire]
for
G_{1}
, by taking (with
loc. cit.
notations)
y=a_{\circ}+\nu(m)
,
x=a_{\circ},n=m^{\prime}-m\in\Lambda_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
,
C=\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}
and
D=\mathcal{C}^{-}
our fixed negative chamber. Then, the assumption
y\in x+\mathcal{C}^{-}
is fulfilled because
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-}
.
Therefore, we have

y+\nu(m^{\prime}-m)=a_{\circ}+\nu(m^{\prime})\leq_{D}y=a_{\circ}+\nu(m).

The order
\leq_{D}
in
loc. cit.
15
15

15

We refer to
[
Bou68
,
n^{\circ}
1 §6 p. 155]
for more details on the order used in
[
BT72
, 4.3.16 Corollaire]
.
translates here to
\nu(m-m^{\prime})=\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta}n_{\alpha}\alpha^{\vee},n_{\alpha}\geq 0
.
∎

Second proof of Theorem
5.2.1
.

This resembles the proof in
[
HR10
, §10.2]
for the untwisted Satake transform with coefficients in
\mathds{C}
, which remains morally valid in our twisted context, although we deal with the ”lexicographic” order on
\Lambda_{M}^{-}
in a different manner.
We need to show that the following morphism of
\mathds{Z}
-modules

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{K}({\mathds{Z}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}^{\dot{W}}\cdot v_{1,K},h}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)*_{K}v_{1,K}}

is ”upper triangular with respect to some total order, with invertible diagonals”.
The proof is similar to the proof of Theorem
4.2.3
.

Recall that
\{h_{x}\colon x\in\Lambda_{M}^{-}\}
forms a
\mathds{Z}
-basis for
\mathcal{H}_{K}(\mathds{Z})
, we also have a
\mathds{Z}
-basis for
\mathcal{R}^{\dot{W}}\cdot v_{1,K}
given by
\{r_{m}\cdot v_{1,K}\colon m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-}\}
.
Fix an element
x\in\Lambda_{M}^{-}
, we have

v_{1,K}*_{K}h_{x}=\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h_{x})\cdot v_{1,K}=\sum_{m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-}}s_{x,m}r_{m},\text{ where }s_{x,m}=|U^{+}m^{-1}K\cap Kx^{-1}K|_{K}\geq 0.

For
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-}
,
r_{m}
appears in the above summation, i.e.
s_{x,m}\neq 0
if and only if
U^{+}m\cap KxK\neq\emptyset
.
Accordingly, by Lemma
5.2.7
,
we obtain

v_{1,K}*_{K}h_{x}=\sum_{m\precsim x}s_{x,m}r_{m}.

Therefore, since the monoid
\Lambda_{M}^{-}
is countable and any element
x\in\Lambda_{M}^{-}
has only finitely many predecessors with respect to the partial order
\precsim
, there exists a lexicographic (total) ordering
x_{1},x_{2},\cdots
for the elements of
\Lambda_{M}^{-}
.
The above discussion shows then, that the matrix of the transformation
h\mapsto\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h)
with respect to the bases
\{h_{x_{i}}\}_{1}^{\infty}
and
\{{x_{i}}\}_{1}^{\infty}
is upper triangular.

Finally, note that the diagonals
s_{x,x}=|U^{+}x^{-1}K\cap Kx^{-1}K|_{K}
are equal to
1
by Lemma
5.2.6
.
This observation shows that the triangular matrix
\mathcal{S}=\left(s_{x,m}\right)_{x,m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-}}
is indeed invertible, which concludes the combinatorial proof of Theorem
5.2.1
.
∎

Remark 5.2.8
.

By the previous proof, for any
m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-}
, we have
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h_{m})=\sum_{m^{\prime}\precsim m}s_{m,m^{\prime}}r_{m^{\prime}}
.
In particular, if
m
is minuscule, hence minimal with respect to the partial order
\precsim
, then
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h_{m})=r_{m}
.
This yields an alternative proof (and generalizes) for Kottwitz’s lemmas
[
Kot84
, Lemma 2.3.3 & Lemma 2.3.7 (b)]
.

Remark 5.2.9

(Comparison of the untwisted and twisted Satake isomorphisms)
.

Consider the
\mathds{C}
-linear map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{B}\colon\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{C};m\otimes c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{B}(m)^{1/2}m\otimes c.}

Observe that for any
m\in\Lambda_{M}
, we have
\eta_{B}(\sum_{w\in W/W_{m}}w(m)={\delta(m)^{1/2}}r_{m}
.
Hence,
\eta_{B}\colon\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{C}\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{C}
is actually an isomorphism of
\mathds{C}
-algebras with
W
-action where the target is endowed with the dot-action (Definition
3.16.1
) and the source with the usual action. Let
\mathcal{S}_{M}^{G}\colon\mathcal{H}_{K}(\mathds{C})\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{C}
be the ”standard” Satake homomorphism
[
HR10
, §9.2]
, then we have

\eta_{B}\circ\mathcal{S}_{M}^{G}=\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}\otimes\text{id}_{\mathds{C}}.

5.3.
Positivity properties of the Satake isomorphism

In this subsection we show that the natural generalization of Rapoport’s positivity result for unramified groups
[
Rap00
, Theorem 1.1]
and Haines listed properties
[
Hai00
, Theorem 1.2]
remains true in general.

Theorem 5.3.1

For any
x\in\Lambda_{M}^{-}
, write
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h_{x})=\sum_{m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-}}s_{x,m}r_{m}
. The following statements are equivalent

(1)

s_{x,m}>0
,

(2)

U^{+}m\cap KxK\neq\emptyset
,

(3)

U^{+}m\cap Iwxw^{\prime}I\neq\emptyset
, for some
w,w^{\prime}\in W_{a_{\circ}}
,

(4)

{x}\succsim{m}
.

Proof.

The equivalence between (1) and (2) was already established while proving Theorem
5.2.1
, in which it is also proved (1)
\Rightarrow
(4).
The equivalence between (2) and (3) is clear from Lemma
3.9.1
.

Now, we show (4)
\Rightarrow
(1): C. Schwer proved in
[
Sch06
]
such an implication for the case of extended affine Weyl groups attached to root data. We shall apply his result to
\mathcal{D}:=({\Lambda}_{M}^{1},\Sigma^{1,\vee},{\Lambda}_{M}^{1,\vee},\Sigma^{1},\Delta^{1,\vee})
.
As we saw in Remark
5.1.6
, the following diagram is commutative
\left(\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}(h_{x})\right)^{1}=\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G^{1}}\iota^{1}(h_{x})
, hence

\sum_{m\precsim x}s_{x,m}r_{m^{1}}=\sum_{y\precsim^{1}x^{1}}s_{x^{1},y}r_{y}\in{\mathcal{R}^{1}}^{\dot{W}}

By Remark
5.2.5
,
m\precsim x
if and only if
{m}^{1}\precsim{x}^{1}
.
Accordingly,
\sum_{m\precsim x}s_{x,m}r_{m^{1}}=\sum_{m\precsim x}s_{x^{1},m^{1}}r_{m^{1}}\in{\mathcal{R}^{1}}^{\dot{W}}
and so
s_{x,m}=s_{x^{1},m^{1}}
, for all
m\precsim x
.
We can actually see it directly; because
M^{1}
normalizes
K
and
x-m\in\Lambda_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
, we get

s_{x^{1},m^{1}}=|(U^{+}K\cap mKx^{-1}K^{1})K^{1}|_{K^{1}}=|(U^{+}K\cap mKx^{-1}K)K^{1}|_{K^{1}}=s_{x,m}.

Thanks to
[
Sch06
, Corollary 4.8]
, we have the implication
x^{1}\precsim m^{1}\Rightarrow s_{m^{1},x^{1}}>0
, which ends the proof of the theorem.
∎

6.
A compressible algebra and centers of parahoric-Hecke algebras

6.1.
Normalized Intertwiners

The dot-action of
W
on
{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}
stabilizes
{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}
,
hence it extends to a dot-action of
W
on
\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
.
Now, since
W
action’s is locally finite, we have by
[
Bou64
, §1 n
∘
9, Proposition 23]
two canonical isomorphisms:

\mathcal{L}^{\flat}:=\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\simeq{\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}}\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\text{ and }{\mathcal{L}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\simeq{\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}}{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}

Tensoring the decomposition provided by Corollary
4.3.6
with
{\mathcal{L}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}
over
{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}
, yields

{\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}}\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})=\bigoplus_{w\in W_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}}i_{w}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}\mathcal{L}^{\flat}=\bigoplus_{w\in W}\mathcal{L}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w}^{\flat}.

(2)

Lemma 6.1.1

For any
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\flat}
, and
s\in\mathcal{S}^{\flat}
we have

f*_{I^{\flat}}(i_{s}^{\flat}+1)-(i_{s}^{\flat}+1)*_{I^{\flat}}\dot{s}(f)=(f-\dot{s}(f))*_{I^{\flat}}\mathcal{G}(\alpha).

Proof.

It suffices to show this statement for
f\in\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
, which follows readily from Proposition
4.4.3
.
∎

For any
s\in\mathcal{T}_{a_{\circ}}
, set

{k}_{s}^{\flat}:=-1+({i}_{s}^{\flat}+1)\mathcal{G}(\alpha)^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}.

Proposition 6.1.2

(1)

There exists a unique group homomorphism

\mathcal{K}^{\flat}\colon\dot{W}_{a_{\circ}}\hookrightarrow\left(\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})\right)^{\times}

such that
\mathcal{K}^{\flat}(s)={k}_{s}^{\flat}
for all
s\in\mathcal{T}_{a_{\circ}}
.

(2)

For any
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\flat}
, we have
f*_{{I}^{\flat}}{k}_{w}^{\flat}={k}_{w}^{\flat}*_{{I}^{\flat}}\dot{w}^{-1}(f)
.

(3)

\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}\mathcal{H}_{{I}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})=\bigoplus_{w\in W}\mathcal{L}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}{k}_{w}^{\flat}=\bigoplus_{w\in W}{k}_{w}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}\mathcal{L}^{\flat}
.

Proof.

(a) The condition forces the image of
\mathcal{K}
to be in
\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\Lambda_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}}\mathcal{H}_{{}^{\flat}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})=\bigoplus_{w\in W}\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}i_{w}^{\flat}
.
The existence and unicity follows then from
[
Lus89
, Proposition 5.2 (a)]
applied to
\mathcal{H}_{{I}^{\flat}}^{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})\simeq\mathcal{H}({W}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}},\mathcal{D}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}},v=q,L)
.

(b) Because
\mathcal{K}
is a morphism of groups, it suffices to check the statement (b) for
w=s\in\mathcal{T}_{a_{\circ}}^{\flat}
, which follows from Lemma
6.1.1
and the definition of
{k}_{s}^{\flat}
.

(c) We proceed as in the proof of
[
Lus89
, Proposition 5.5 (a)]
.
Let
X=\sum_{w\in W}\mathcal{L}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}{k}_{w}
.
Since
W_{a_{\circ}}
is generated by
\mathcal{T}_{a_{\circ}}
, it suffices to show that
i_{s}^{\flat}\in X
, for all
s\in\mathcal{T}_{a_{\circ}}
.
But, by definition, we have
{i}_{s}^{\flat}\in\mathcal{L}^{\flat}(1+{k}_{s}^{\flat})
.
Therefore,
\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\Lambda_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}}\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}^{\flat}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})=X
.
We saw in (
2
) p.
2
, that
\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
is free over
\mathcal{L}
of rank
|W|
.
Therefore,
\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})=\oplus_{w\in W}\mathcal{L}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}{k}_{w}^{\flat}
.
The other equality is proved in a similar way.
∎

6.2.
A skew group ring

Proposition 6.2.1

Let
\mathcal{L}^{\flat}*\dot{W}
be the skew group ring of
\dot{W}
over
\mathcal{L}^{\flat}
(See Definition
.1
).
We have an isomorphism of
\mathcal{L}^{\flat}
-algebras:

\mathcal{L}^{\flat}*\dot{W}\to\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}\mathcal{H}_{{I}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}),r\cdot w\mapsto r*_{I^{\flat}}{k}_{w}^{\flat}.

Proof.

This follows readily from (b) and (c) of Proposition
6.1.2
.
∎

6.3.
An Azumaya algebra

Lemma 6.3.1

(1)

The skew group ring
\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}*\dot{W}
is the matrix algebra
\mathbb{M}_{|W|}(\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\dot{W}})
.

(2)

The two ring extensions
\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}/\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\dot{W}}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\flat}/{\mathcal{L}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}
are
{\dot{W}}
-Galois.

Proof.

(i) There is an obvious map of algebras

\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}*{\dot{W}}\to End_{\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\dot{W}}}(\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}})

where, an element
a\in\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
is taken to multiplication by
a
on
\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
, and
w\in{\dot{W}}
is taken to the obvious map
w:\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}\to\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}
.
By an argument similar to the proof of linear independence of characters, one can check that this map is injective. Now since each side has dimension
|W|^{2}
as vector space over
\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\dot{W}}
, this map is an isomorphism.

(ii) We deduce from (i) that
\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}*{\dot{W}}
is Azumaya over
\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\dot{W}}
. Accordingly, Theorem
.4
shows that
\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}/\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\dot{W}}
is
{\dot{W}}
-Galois, and so is
\mathcal{L}^{\flat}/{\mathcal{L}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}
using Corollary
.1
.
∎

Proposition 6.3.2

The
{\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
-algebra
A=\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}\mathcal{H}_{{I}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})
is compressible, that is

Z(eAe)=eZ(A)=e\mathcal{L}^{\dot{W}}

for any idempotent
e
of
A
.

Proof.

By Theorem
.4
and Lemma
6.3.1
(ii), the algebra
\mathcal{L}^{\flat}*\dot{W}
is Azumaya over
\mathcal{L}^{{\flat}}
, so the proposition is a consequence of Theorem
.1
.
∎

6.4.
The center of parahoric–Hecke algebras

Theorem 6.4.1

For any facet
\mathcal{F}\subset\overline{\mathfrak{a}}
, we have

Z\left(\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})\right)=\dot{\Theta}_{\text{Bern}}^{\flat}({\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])*_{I^{\flat}}{\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}.

In particular, the set
\{r_{m}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}{\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}:m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat}\}
forms a basis for the
\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]
-module
Z\left(\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})\right)
.

Lemma 6.4.2

For any idempotent
e\in\mathcal{H}_{{I}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Q}})
, we have

Z(e({\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}}\mathcal{H}_{{I}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Q}}))e)=e{\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{e{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}}Z(e(\mathcal{H}_{{I}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Q}}))e).

Proof.

Given Corollary
4.5.1
, we have an inclusion
Z(e({\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}}\mathcal{H}_{{I}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Q}}))e)\supset e{\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{e{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}}Z(e(\mathcal{H}_{{I}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Q}}))e).

The opposite one is true because:

(i) The morphism
e\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})e\to e{\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{e{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}}e\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})e
,
h\mapsto e\otimes h
is injective.
This follows readily from the proof of Lemma
4.4.1
, in which we proved that
\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])
is
{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}
-torsion free, so in particular
e\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Q}})e
is also
e{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}
-torsion free and
{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\simeq e{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}
.

(ii) Any element in the left hand side can be written as
\ell\otimes h
, for some
\ell\in e{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}
and
h\in e\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})e
, hence we must have
h\in Z(e\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]})e)
. This shows the lemma.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subset\overline{\mathfrak{a}}
be any facet and
e_{\mathcal{F}}
the idempotent
[K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}:I^{\flat}]{\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}
.
By Proposition
6.3.2
and Lemma
6.4.2
, we have

e_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}\mathcal{L}^{\dot{W}}=e_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}{\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{e_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}}e_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}=e_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathcal{L}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{e_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathcal{R}_{\textit{\emph{\text{aff}}}}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}}Z(e_{\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Q}})e_{\mathcal{F}}).

For any
h\in Z(e_{\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Q}})e_{\mathcal{F}})
, there are then
s,r\in{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}
such that
sh-re=0
.
We have then an equality
sv_{1,\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}*_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}h=rv_{1,\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}
.
By Propoisition
4.1.4
, we deduce that
v_{1,\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}*_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}h=tv_{1,\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}
for some
t\in{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes\mathds{Q}
, this shows
v_{1}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}h=v_{1}^{\flat}t*_{I^{\flat}}e_{\mathcal{F}}
and accordingly
h=t*_{I^{\flat}}e_{\mathcal{F}}
by Corollary
4.2.4
.
In conclusion,

{\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}*_{I}({\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Q})=Z(e_{\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}({\mathds{Q}})e_{\mathcal{F}})=Z(\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Q}})).

Let
h\in Z(\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}))
, by the previous discussion, there exists
a/b\in\mathds{Q}
and
r\in{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}
such that
h=\frac{a}{b}{\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}*_{I^{\flat}}\dot{\Theta}_{Bern}^{\flat}(r)
, but by definition the term
{\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}*_{I^{\flat}}\dot{\Theta}_{Bern}^{\flat}(r)\in\mathcal{H}_{I^{\flat}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}])
, hence one must have
\frac{a}{b}\in\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]
. Consequently, we have

\displaystyle Z(\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}))

\displaystyle={\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}*_{I^{\flat}}\dot{\Theta}_{Bern}^{\flat}({\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]).\qed

The linear independence of the elements
r_{m}^{\flat}*_{I^{\flat}}{\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}(m\in\Lambda_{M}^{-,\flat})
is clear from the injectivity of
-*_{I^{\flat}}{\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}^{\flat}}
and Corollary
4.3.6
.

6.5.
Compatibility of the twisted Satake and twisted Bernstein isomorphisms

From the above, we see that
Z(\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}))
is a free
\mathcal{R}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]
-module of rank one, with basis the canonical vector
{\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}}
.
Define
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{F}}\colon Z(\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}}({\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}))\to\mathcal{R}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]
, characterized by
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{F}}(h)*_{I}{\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}}=h
.
By Lemma
5.2.3
, we have
\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{F}}=\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{M}^{G}
if
\mathcal{F}=\{a_{\circ}\}
.
The Satake and Bernstein twisted isomorphisms are compatible in the following sense:

Theorem 6.5.1

Let
\mathcal{F}\subset\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mathfrak{a}}
, be two facets, i.e.
K_{\mathcal{F}}\supset K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}\supset I
.
The following diagram of
\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]
-algebras is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}}^{\dot{W}}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\mathcal{H}_{K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\mathcal{H}_{I}(\mathds{Z}[q^{-1}]))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\Theta}_{Bern}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-*_{K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}}{\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-*_{I}{\bf 1}_{K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Proof.

Similar to the proof of Lemma
5.2.2
. ∎

Remark 6.5.2
.

Given Theorem
5.2.1
,
we have, in particular, an inclusion
Z(\mathcal{H}_{{I^{\flat}}}(\mathds{Z}))\subset{\dot{\Theta}_{\text{\emph{Bern}}}}({\mathcal{R}^{\dot{W}}})
.

Appendix. Skew group rings

This appendix is devoted to some properties of
G
-Galois extensions of commutative rings.

.6.
G
-Galois extensions of commutative rings

Definition .1
.

Let
S
be a commutative ring and
G
a finite group of ring automorphisms of
S
. Write
S*G
for the free
S
-module with basis
\{g\in G\}
.
We make
S*G
into a ring as follows: for
s,s^{\prime}\in S
and
g,g^{\prime}\in G
, define
(s\cdot g)(s^{\prime}\cdot g^{\prime})=sg(s^{\prime})\cdot gg^{\prime}
.
The ring
S*G
is usually called a skew group ring or a trivial crossed product.

Definition .2
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring. Two
R
-algebras homomorphisms
f,g\colon A\to B
are said to be strongly distinct if for every nonzero idempotent
e\in B
there exists
x\in A
such that
f(x)e\neq g(x)e
.

Remark .3
.

Strong distinctness is equivalent to Dedekind’s lemma in the context of separable algebras. The elements of
G
are all pairwise strongly distinct if and only if
G
is free over
S
in
\text{End}_{R}(S)

[
FP97
, Proposition 2.1]
.

Theorem .4

Let
S
be a commutative ring extension and
G
a finite group of ring automorphisms of
S
. Set
R:=S^{G}
.
The ring
S
is called a Galois extension of
R
with Galois group
G
or
S/R
is a
G
-Galois ring extension, if one of the following equivalent statements is satisfied:

(1)

S
is separable over
R
and the elements of
G
are all pairwise strongly distinct.

(2)

There exist elements
x_{i},y_{i}
in
S
,
1\leq i\leq n
for some integer
n
, such that
\sum_{i}x_{i}g(y_{i})=\delta_{1,g}\mathbf{1}_{S}
, for all
g\in G
. The set
\{x_{i},y_{i}\colon 1\leq i\leq n\}
is called a
G
-Galois system for
S
.

(3)

S
is a finitely generated projective
R
-module and the map
\varphi:S*G\to\text{End}_{R}(S)
, given by
\varphi(xg)(y)=xg(y)
, is an isomorphism of
S
-modules and
R
-algebras.

(4)

For any left
S*G
-module
M
the map
\mu\colon S\otimes_{R}M^{G}\to M,(s\otimes m)\to sm
is an isomorphism of
S
-modules.

(5)

The map
S\otimes_{R}S\to\prod_{g\in G}S,(x,y)\mapsto(xg(y))_{g\in G}

is an isomorphism of
S
-algebras.

(6)

For each maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
S
and each
1\neq g\in G
there exists
y\in S
such that
g(y)-y\not\in\mathfrak{m}
.

(7)

S\pi S=S*G
where
\pi=\sum_{g\in G}g
.

(8)

S*G
is Azumaya over
R
.

(9)

S*G
is H-separable over
S
.

When this is the case,
S
is a maximal commutative
R
-subalgebra of
S*G
with
(S*G)\otimes_{R}S\simeq\mathbb{M}_{|G|}(S)
and
S\otimes_{R}(S*G)\simeq\mathbb{M}_{|G|}(S)
.

Proof.

The equivalence of the first seven statement is
[
CHR65
, Theorem 1.3]
.
For (8), first note that
S
can be seen as an
(S;R)
-subbimodule of
S*G
via
x\to x\pi
, hence the notation
S\pi S
makes sense. Because
\pi g=\pi
we see that
S\pi S
is a two sided ideal of
S*G
, hence
S\pi S=S*G
if and only if
\mathbf{1}_{S}\in S\pi S
, which is equivalent to the existence of
x_{i},y_{i}
in
S
,
1\leq i\leq n
for some integer
n
, such that
\mathbf{1}_{S}=\sum_{i}x_{i}\pi y_{i}=\sum_{g\in G}(\sum_{i}x_{i}g(y_{i}))g
, i.e.
\sum_{i}x_{i}g(y_{i})=\delta_{1,g}\mathbf{1}_{S}
, for all
g\in G
.
For 8 and 9, this is
[
Ike81
, Theorem 2]
or
[
AS95
, Corollary 2]
.
∎

Remark .5
.

If
S/R
is
G
-Galois, then
\text{Rank}_{R}S=|G|

[
FP97
, Lemma 2.3]
.

.7.
Properties

Proposition .1

For any
G
-Galois extension
S/R
:

(i)
S
is faithfully flat over
R
.

(ii)
\text{Tr}\colon S\to R
is surjective.

(iii) The
R
-submodule
R
of
S
is a direct summand of
S
.

(iv) For any
R
-algebra
T
,
T\otimes_{R}S/S
is a

G
-Galois extension iff
S/R
is
G
-Galois.

(v) Let
S/R
and
S^{\prime}/R
be
G
-Galois. Then every
G
-equivariant
R
-algebra homomorphism
f:S\to S^{\prime}
of

G
-Galois extensions is an isomorphism.

Proof.

See
[
Gre92
, Chapitre 0, §1]
.
∎

.8.
An obvious corollary

Corollary .1

Let
T
be a commutative ring with a unity
\mathbf{1}_{T}
and
G
be a finite group
G
that acts faithfully on it by ring automorphisms. Let
S\subset T
be a subring such that
\mathbf{1}_{T}=\mathbf{1}_{S}
. If
S/S^{G}
is
G
-Galois then so is
T/T^{G}
.

Proof.

Thanks to Theorem
.4
, there exist elements
x_{i},y_{i}
in
S\subset T
,
1\leq i\leq n
, such that
\sum_{i}x_{i}g(y_{i})=\delta_{1,g}\mathbf{1}_{S}
, for all
g\in G
. The corollary follows readily since
\mathbf{1}_{T}=\mathbf{1}_{S}
.
∎

.9.
Compressibility

Theorem .1

If
A
is an Azumaya algebra over a commutative ring
R
then it is also compressible that is if
Z(eAe)=eZ(A)=eR
for each idempotent
e
of
A
.

Proof.

This is
[
AP84
, Theorem 2]
.
∎

Example .2
.

If
S/R
is
G
-Galois then
S*G
is compressible.
